On the Mixtures and Powers of Simple Drugs

De simplicium medicamentorum temperamentis ac facultatibus · Kühn XI 379-892 and XII 1-377 · books 1-11

No complete published translation into English by a scholar that we know of. An English translation of books 1-5 by J. Wilkins is announced in the Cambridge Galen Translations. Books 6-8: a French translation with the Greek by C. Manco (doctoral thesis, Montpellier and Bologna, 2020). The prefaces of books 6-11 and the first two entries of book 6: English by M. Coughlin (ancientmedicine.org, 2023). All eleven books: a free French version by H. Fayard (1548). An unreviewed machine translation into English of Kühn XI and XII, the volumes containing these books, was posted at Source Library (sourcelibrary.org) during 2026. This is rendering A, made by a language model and not yet checked by a scholar. The small grey numbers are the pages of Kühn's Greek edition in books 1-5 and the entry numbers (book.chapter.entry) in books 6-11. Passages with a red rule failed the machine checks; those checks are automated agreement tests, not a reading of the Greek, so a passage without the rule is not checked either. Words in square brackets were supplied by the model for the sense; Greek in parentheses gives the original word; any remark about a doubtful or corrupt reading is the model's, not a scholar's.

Check (a): the English back-translated into Greek by a third model shares at least half of the unit's term lemmas with the source. Check (b): the two renderings agree on at least 70% of the unit's target terms. Check (c): a model judge, salted with 10 planted errors and 10 anchors it had to get right, finds at least 80% of the term renderings within the LSJ entry's senses, with at most 2 outside. A unit fails when it fails two of three.

Flags, under each passage: places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors. Sentence by sentence, each rendering is compared with the Greek for numbers, negations and words of more or less, and for the names of drugs and parts of the body (a name in the English with none in the Greek, or a name in the Greek with none in the English). These are word-matching tests: a flag often comes from a sentence pair that is aligned a little off, a paraphrase or a name the model kept in Greek, and a passage with no flag is not checked by them either. The flags play no part in the unit verdicts.

K11.619-620K. XI 619–620, ch. 4.1 difficulty mid (1.55/100; thin 2, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 13/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ἐπειδὴ δὲ καὶ μαλακτικόν τι λέγεται καὶ χαλαστικὸν, ἀραιωτικόν τε καὶ πυκνωτικὸν, ἐμπλαστικόν τε καὶ διαφορητικὸν, ἕτερά τε πάμπολλα παραπλήσια τούτοις, κάλλιον ἂν εἴη καὶ περὶ τῶν τοιούτων διελθεῖν· ἅμα δὲ αὐτοῖς, ἢ καὶ πρὸ αὐτῶν ἔτι, καὶ περὶ τῆς 620 τῶν χυμῶν ἁπάντων οὐσίας τε ἅμα καὶ δυνάμεως, ὑπὲρ ἧς εἴρηται μὲν κᾀν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων ἐπὶ τῆς τελευτῆς, ὁπότε καὶ τὴν τοῦ Πλάτωνος ἐκ Τιμαίου παρεθέμην ῥῆσιν· ἀλλ’ ἐκεῖ μὲν, ἕνεκα τοῦ τῶν αἰσθητῶν ἀναμνῆσαι παθῶν, ὧν ἡ γλῶττα πέφυκε καθ’ ἕκαστον αὐτῶν πάσχειν, ἐνταυθοῖ δὲ περὶ τῆς οὐσίας τε καὶ δυνάμεως ὁ λόγος ἔσται μοι. πάσχει μὲν γὰρ δήπου καὶ τἄλλα τοῦ ζώου μόρια τῇ γλώττῃ παραπλησίως, οὐ μὴν ἀκριβῶς αἰσθάνεται τῶν παθῶν, ἡ δέ γε πρὸς τῷ πάσχειν ἔτι καὶ τὸ τῆς αἰσθήσεως ἀκριβὲς κεκτημένη διαγνωστικὴ τῆς φύσεως ἁπάντων τούτων ἐστίν. οὐ καὶ δὴ τότε θερμὸν, ἢ ψυχρὸν, ἢ ξηρὸν, ἢ ὑγρὸν τὴν μὲν γλῶτταν ἤτοι ψύχειν, ἢ θερμαίνειν, ἢ ξηραίνειν, ἢ ὑγραίνειν, οὐχὶ δέ γε καὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἕκαστον πέφυκεν, ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ ταύτην, οὕτω καὶ τἄλλα διατίθησί τε καὶ ἀλλοιοῖ κατὰ τὸ μᾶλλον καὶ ἧττον ἐν αὐτοῖς γιγνομένης τῆς διαφορᾶς, οὐ κατὰ τὸ πάσχειν, ἢ μὴ πάσχειν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὐ καὶ δὴ τότε, χυμῶν
But since something is also called softening and relaxing, rarefying and condensing, clogging and dispersing, and very many others similar to these, it would be better to go through such things too; and together with them, or even before them, also the 620 substance and at the same time the power of all the flavours, about which something has been said at the end of the first of these commentaries, when I also set out the passage of Plato from the Timaeus; but there it was for the sake of recalling the perceptible affections which the tongue is naturally disposed to undergo in the case of each of them, whereas here my account will be about their substance and power. For surely the other parts of the animal too are affected similarly to the tongue, yet they do not perceive the affections accurately; whereas the tongue, possessing, in addition to being affected, also accuracy of sensation, is capable of discerning the nature of all these. For indeed it is not the case (οὐ καὶ δὴ τότε) that what is hot or cold or dry or wet is naturally such as either to cool or heat or dry or moisten the tongue, but not also each of the other parts; rather, just as it disposes and alters this one, so too it disposes and alters the others, the difference among them arising in respect of more and less, not in respect of being affected or not being affected.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὐ καὶ δὴ τότε (apparently corrupt; translated as if οὐ γὰρ δὴ τὸ), χυμῶν (rendered 'flavours'), ἐνταυθοῖ
Since, however, a drug is also said to be softening and relaxing, rarefying and condensing, clogging and dispersing, and very many other things similar to these, it would be better to go through such things too. Together with them, or even still before them, I should also treat the 620 substance and at the same time the power of all the flavours (χυμοί). Something was said about this in the first of these commentaries too, at the end, when I also quoted the passage of Plato from the Timaeus. But there it was for the sake of recalling the perceptible affections which the tongue is naturally disposed to undergo in respect of each of them, whereas here my discourse will be about their substance and power. For the other parts of the animal are, of course, also affected in a way similar to the tongue, yet they do not perceive the affections precisely. The tongue, however, besides being affected, also possesses precision of sensation, and so it is capable of discerning the nature of all these things. For it is not the case that what is hot, or cold, or dry, or moist is naturally disposed to cool, or heat, or dry, or moisten the tongue, but not each of the other parts as well. Rather, just as it disposes and alters the tongue, so it also disposes and alters the other parts, the difference arising in them according to the more and the less, not according to being affected or not being affected.

K11.620-621K. XI 620–621, ch. 4.1, 4.2 difficulty high (3.28/100; thin 0, absent 4) (a) pass (b) pass 6/6 (c) pass 12/12 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ὅτι μὲν γὰρ καὶ ξηροῦ καὶ ὑγροῦ καὶ θερμοῦ καὶ ψυχροῦ σώματος οὐδὲν ἧττον ἁπάντων τῶν ἄλλων τοῦ ζώου μορίων ἡ γλῶττα πέφυκεν 621 εἶναι διαγνωστικὴ πρόδηλον παντὶ, πικρότητας δὲ καὶ γλυκύτητας, ἁλυκότητάς τε καὶ ὀξύτητας, αὐστηρότητάς τε καὶ στρυφνότητας καὶ δριμύτητας, αὕτη μόνη διακρίνειν πεπίστευται, καίτοι τήν γε ὁμοιότητα τῶν παθῶν ἐστιν εὑρεῖν κᾀν τοῖς ἄλλοις ἅπασι μέρεσιν, καὶ μάλιστα ἐν τοῖς ἡλκωμένοις. ἡ γάρ τοι τοῦ δέρματος σκληρότης καὶ πυκνότης ἀποστέγει καὶ ἀπομάχεται καὶ κωλύει διέρχεσθαι πρὸς τὴν σάρκα τὰς τῶν ἔξωθεν αὐτῇ προσπιπτόντων δυνάμεις. Οὕτω γὰρ εἴρηται κᾀκεῖνο πρὸς Ἱπποκράτους, ἕλκεσι τὸ μὲν ψυχρὸν δακνῶδες, καίτοι γε τῷ δέρματι πρὶν ἑλκωθῆναι δακνῶδες οὐκ ἦν. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ὀφθαλμοῖσι καὶ μυκτῆρσι καὶ ἐν τῷ στόματι μέρεσι τοῦ ζώου, δακνῶδες τὸ ψυχρόν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For that the tongue is by nature capable of discerning a dry and a wet and a hot and a cold body no less than all the other parts of the animal 621 is obvious to everyone; but bitternesses and sweetnesses, saltinesses and sournesses, harshnesses and acerbities and pungencies, it alone is believed to distinguish, although the similarity of the affections can be found in all the other parts as well, and especially in those that are ulcerated. For indeed the hardness and density of the skin shuts out and resists and prevents the powers of the things falling upon it from outside from passing through to the flesh. For thus too that saying was spoken by Hippocrates, that 'to ulcers the cold is biting', although to the skin, before it was ulcerated, it was not biting. And the cold is biting also to the eyes and to the nostrils and to the parts of the animal in the mouth.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: αὐτῇ (referent unclear: the skin, its hardness, or the flesh), ἐν τῷ στόματι μέρεσι (syntax loose; taken as 'the parts in the mouth'), ὀξύτητας (rendered 'sourness')
For it is plain to everyone that the tongue is by nature 621 able to discern a dry and a moist, a hot and a cold body no less than all the other parts of the animal; but bitterness and sweetness, saltiness and sourness, harshness, acerbity and pungency it alone is believed to distinguish, although one can find the similarity of the affections in all the other parts as well, and especially in those that are ulcerated. For the hardness and density of the skin shuts out and fights off, and prevents from passing through to the flesh, the powers of the things that fall upon it from outside. For it is in this sense that Hippocrates also made that statement: 'to ulcers cold is biting', although to the skin before it was ulcerated it was not biting. And to the eyes too, and to the nostrils, and to the parts of the animal in the mouth, cold is biting.

K11.621-622K. XI 621–622, ch. 4.2 difficulty mid (2.12/100; thin 1, absent 3) (a) fail (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
οὐχ ἥκιστα δὲ γαστρός τε καὶ ἐντέρων τοῖς ἔνδον, ὥστε καὶ χαλεπὸν εἶναι διακρῖναι πολλάκις εἴτε κατεψυγμένος τις, εἴτε καὶ διὰ χυμοῦ δριμύτητα δάκνοιτο, συμβαίνει δὲ ταῦτα εὐλόγως· ἐπειδὴ γὰρ τὸ ψυχρὸν, ὧν ἂν ψαύσῃ, συνάγει τε τὴν οὐσίαν αὐτῶν καὶ πυκνοῖ καὶ σφίγγει, πρῶτον μὲν ἀναγκαῖον ἐν τῷδε τὰς λεπτὰς ὑγρότητας ἐκκρίνεσθαι, 622 καθάπερ ἐξ ὀφθαλμῶν μὲν τὸ δάκρυον, ἐκ δὲ τῶν ῥινῶν τὴν κόρυζαν, ἔπειτα δὲ καὶ δακνῶδες αὐτοῖς φαίνεται, καθ’ ἕτερον μὲν τρόπον τοῦ θερμοῦ. οὐ γὰρ τῷ διαβιβρώσκειν τὸ συνεχὲς, ἀλλὰ τῷ διασπᾷν ἀνιαρόν θ’ ἅμα καὶ δακνῶδες ὑπάρχει τὸ ψυχρὸν, ὅθεν οὐδὲ τοῖς σκληροῖς σώμασι φαίνεται τοιοῦτον. ἐν γὰρ τῷ συνάγεσθαι καὶ θλίβεσθαι τὰ μαλακὰ, τό θ’ ὑγρὸν αὐτῶν ἐκπιέζεσθαι συμβαίνει καὶ διασπᾶσθαι τὰ μόρια, τὸ μὲν ὑγρὸν ὡς ἐν τῇ συναγομένῃ τε καὶ πρὸς ἡμετέρων χειρῶν θλιβομένῃ σπογγιᾷ, τὸ διασπᾶσθαι δὲ ὡς ἐν τοῖς διαιρουμένοις καὶ διασπωμένοις, εἴτ’ οὖν ὑφ’ ἡμῶν ἑλκόντων ταῖς χερσὶν, εἴθ’ ὑπό τινος ἑτέρου. τοιοῦτον γάρ τι καὶ τοῖς ἀραιοῖς τε καὶ μαλακοῖς σώμασιν ὑπὸ τοῦ ψυχροῦ πάσχειν ἀναγκαῖον, ὑποδεχομένης μὲν εἴσω τὴν ψύξιν τῆς ἀραιότητος ἑτοίμως, ἔτι δὲ ἑτοιμοτέρως τῶν ἐν τῷ βάθει μορίων τῶν μαλακῶν ὑπ’ αὐτῆς πηγνυμένων.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
and not least to the inner parts of the stomach and intestines, so that it is often even difficult to discriminate whether someone is chilled or is being bitten on account of the pungency of a humour; and these things come about with good reason. For since the cold, whatever it touches, draws together the substance of those things and condenses and constricts it, first it is necessary in this process that the thin moistures be excreted, 622 just as the tear from the eyes and the coryza from the nostrils; and then it also appears biting to them, though in a different manner from the hot. For it is not by eroding what is continuous but by tearing it apart that the cold is at once painful and biting; whence it does not appear such to hard bodies either. For when soft bodies are drawn together and compressed, it happens both that their moisture is squeezed out and that their parts are torn apart: the moisture, as in a sponge that is drawn together and compressed by our hands; the being torn apart, as in things that are divided and pulled apart, whether by us dragging with our hands or by something else. For it is necessary that rare and soft bodies too undergo something of this sort from the cold, since the rarity readily admits the cooling within, and the soft parts in the depth are still more readily congealed by it.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τοῖς ἔνδον, τὸ συνεχὲς, ἑτοιμοτέρως, πηγνυμένων
and not least to the inner parts of the stomach and intestines, so that it is often difficult to distinguish whether someone has been chilled or is being bitten through the pungency of a humour. These things happen with good reason. For since the cold draws together the substance of whatever it touches, and condenses and constricts it, it is necessary, first, that in this process the thin fluids are excreted, 622 as tears from the eyes and coryza from the nostrils; and then it also appears biting to these parts, though in a different manner from the hot. For it is not by eating through what is continuous, but by tearing it apart, that the cold is at once painful and biting; hence it does not appear such to hard bodies either. For when soft things are drawn together and compressed, it happens both that their fluid is squeezed out and that their parts are torn apart: the fluid, as in a sponge drawn together and compressed by our hands; the tearing apart, as in things that are divided and torn apart, whether by us pulling with our hands or by something else. For something of this sort must also be suffered by rare and soft bodies under the action of the cold, since their rarity readily admits the chilling within, and the soft parts in the depth are still more readily congealed by it.

Greek (Kühn)
καθ’ ἕκαστον οὖν τῶν πόρων εἰσιὸν τὸ ψυχρὸν ἐν τοῖς ἀραιοῖς σώμασιν, οἷάπερ τά θ’ ἡλκωμένα πάντα ἐστὶ καὶ ὀφθαλμοὶ καὶ μυκτῆρες καὶ στόμα καὶ τὰ κατὰ τὴν γαστέρα, ἐπιλαμβάνει πάντα τῶν ψυχομένων σωμάτων τὰ μόρια καὶ τρόπον τινὰ πολλαῖς 623 ἐν κύκλῳ λαβαῖς αὐτὰ συνάγον καὶ πιλοῦν τῶν συνεχῶν ἀποσπᾷ. χώρας οὖν ἀναγκαῖον ἐν τῷδε τῷ πάθει γίνεσθαι κενὰς οὐκ ὀλίγας, οὐ μόνον τῷ τὰς λεπτὰς ὑγρότητας ἐκκρίνεσθαι πιεζομένας, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῷ τὴν μαλακὴν οὐσίαν εἰς ἐλάττονα τόπον συνάγεσθαι. χώραν δὲ κενὴν ἐν συνεχέσι σώμασιν οὐκ ἐγχωρεῖ συστῆναι φυλαττομένης τῆς συνεχείας. εἴπερ οὖν συνίσταται, δῆλον ὡς φθείρεται μὲν αὕτη, διασπᾶται δὲ τὸ συνεχὲς σῶμα, κᾀν τούτῳ τὸ τῆς αἰσθήσεως ἀνιαρόν τε ἅμα καὶ δακνῶδες ὑπαντᾷ πάθος, οὐ μὴν ἥ γε ποιότης ἀμφοῖν ἡ αὐτὴ, τῶν τε διὰ τὸ ψύχειν δακνόντων ἐστὶν καὶ τῶν διὰ τὸ θερμαίνειν, ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ μὲν τῶν προτέρων εὐθέως καὶ τὸ συνάγεσθαί τε καὶ σφίγγεσθαι τὴν οὐσίαν αἴσθησις ἡμῖν ἐστιν, ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν δευτέρων ἡ τοῦ διαλύεσθαί τε καὶ χεῖσθαι. καὶ δὴ καὶ τὸ τάχος οὐχ ὅμοιον ἐπ’ ἀμφοτέρων τῶν παθῶν, ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ μὲν τῶν ψυχόντων βραδύνει πως μᾶλλον ἡ δῆξις, ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν θερμαινόντων ὠκύτατα διεξέρχεται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Accordingly the cold, entering through each of the pores in rare bodies—such as all ulcerated parts are, and the eyes and nostrils and mouth and the parts about the belly—takes hold of all the parts of the bodies being cooled and, in a certain manner drawing them together and compacting them with many 623 holds all round, tears them away from the parts continuous with them. It is necessary, then, that in this affection not a few empty spaces come to be, not only because the thin moistures, being squeezed, are excreted, but also because the soft substance is drawn together into a smaller place. But an empty space cannot come into being in continuous bodies while their continuity is preserved. If, therefore, one does come into being, it is clear that the continuity is destroyed and the continuous body is torn apart, and in this there arises the affection of sensation that is at once painful and biting. Yet the quality of the two is not the same—of those things that bite by cooling and of those that bite by heating—but in the case of the former we at once also have a sensation of the substance being drawn together and constricted, whereas in the case of the latter one of its being dissolved and diffused. And indeed the speed too is not alike in the two affections; rather, in the case of cooling things the biting is somewhat slower, whereas in the case of heating things it runs through most swiftly.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πιλοῦν, ὑπαντᾷ, χεῖσθαι
Entering, then, through each of the pores in rare bodies (such as are all ulcerated parts, and the eyes, the nostrils, the mouth and the parts about the stomach), the cold lays hold of all the parts of the bodies being cooled and, as it were with many 623 grips all round, gathering them together and compressing them, tears them away from the parts continuous with them. In this affection, then, not a few empty spaces must come to be, not only because the thin fluids are pressed and excreted, but also because the soft substance is drawn together into a smaller place. But an empty space cannot arise in continuous bodies while their continuity is preserved. If, then, one does arise, it is clear that this continuity is destroyed and the continuous body is torn apart; and in this the affection of sensation that is at once distressing and biting presents itself. Yet the quality of both is not the same, of the things that bite by cooling and of those that bite by heating. In the former we at once also have a perception of the substance being drawn together and constricted; in the latter, a perception of its being dissolved and diffused. Moreover, the speed is not alike in the two affections: with cooling things the biting is somehow slower, whereas with heating things it passes through most swiftly.

K11.623-624K. XI 623–624, ch. 4.2 difficulty low (0.0/100; thin 0, absent 0) (a) fail (b) pass 5/7 (c) pass 14/14 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ὁπότ’ οὖν αὐτάρκως ἤδη διώρισται καὶ τῇ φύσει τῶν πραγμάτων αὐτῇ καὶ τῇ πρὸς ἡμᾶς αἰσθήσει, καὶ λέλεκται σαφῶς ﻿ 624 ὅπως μὲν τὸ θερμὸν, ὅπως δὲ τὸ ψυχρὸν δακνῶδες φαίνεται. χρὴ μὲν δήπου καὶ λογίζεσθαί τινα καὶ πρὶν ἀκοῦσαι παρ’ ἡμῶν, ὡς τῶν δακνόντων φαρμάκων τὰ μὲν θερμὰ ταῖς δυνάμεσιν ἀναγκαῖον εἶναι, τὰ δὲ ψυχρά. καὶ παρ’ ἡμῶν δὲ ἐπιμαθεῖν ἐστι τοὺς διορισμοὺς αὐτῶν ἀκριβῶς, ἀναμνησθέντας πρότερον ὡς καὶ τὸ πόμα καὶ τὸ βαλανεῖον, ὅταν εὐκράτως ἔχῃ, θερμὰ λέγομεν, ἐνίοτε καταχρώμενοι τῇ προσηγορίᾳ. εὔκρατα γὰρ ἐχρῆν αὐτὰ λέγεσθαι καὶ σύμμετρα μᾶλλον ἢ θερμά. τὰ μὲν οὖν οὕτω θερμὰ προσηνῆ τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ φίλια. διαχεῖται γὰρ ἡμῶν τὸ συνεστός τε καὶ πεπηγὸς ἐν τοῖς σώμασιν, ἐν ᾧ μάλιστα πάθει τὸ ἥδεσθαι τοῖς ζώοις ἐστίν· χρονίζοντα δὲ καὶ ταῦτα κατ’ ὀλίγον ἀνιαρὰ γίνονται. μεταπίπτει γὰρ εἰς ἀμετρίαν οὕτω γε χύσεως ὡς καὶ διαφορεῖσθαι καὶ διαλύεσθαι καὶ σκεδάννυσθαι τὴν οὐσίαν ἡμῶν. ἀλλὰ κᾀν τούτῳ χωρὶς τοῦ δάκνειν ἀνιᾷ, λειποψυχίαν τε ἐμποιοῦντα καὶ κατάπτωσιν δυνάμεως, ὥστε καὶ τὸν θάνατον ἕπεσθαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Since, then, it has now been sufficiently distinguished, both by the nature of the things themselves and by our perception of them, and it has been clearly stated 624 in what way the hot and in what way the cold appear biting, one ought surely to reason, even before hearing it from us, that of the biting drugs some must necessarily be hot in their powers, others cold. And from us one may further learn the distinctions among them precisely, having first recalled that we call both a drink and a bath, when they are well-tempered, hot, sometimes using the term loosely; for they ought rather to be called well-tempered and moderate than hot. Things that are hot in this way, then, are gentle and friendly; for what is condensed and congealed in our bodies is diffused, and it is in this affection above all that pleasure lies for living beings. But when they persist for a long time, even these gradually become painful; for it passes over into such an excess of diffusion that our substance is dispersed, dissolved and scattered. But even in this case they cause distress without biting, producing faintness and a collapse of the power, so that death too follows.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καταχρώμενοι, δυνάμεως
Since, then, the matter has now been adequately distinguished, both by the very nature of the things and by our perception of them, and it has been stated clearly 624 in what way the hot, and in what way the cold, appears biting, one ought surely to reason out, even before hearing it from us, that of the biting drugs some must be hot in their powers and others cold. One can also learn from us in addition their precise distinctions, once we have first recalled that we call both a drink and a bath 'hot' when they are well-tempered, sometimes using the term loosely. For they ought rather to be called well-tempered and moderate than hot. Things hot in this way, then, are gentle and congenial. For what is compacted and congealed in our bodies is diffused, and it is chiefly in this affection that pleasure lies for animals. But if prolonged, these too gradually become distressing. For the diffusion passes over into such immoderation that our substance is dispersed, dissolved and scattered. Yet even in this case they cause distress without biting, producing fainting and collapse of strength, so that even death follows.

Greek (Kühn)
τὰ δ’ ἔτι μᾶλλον τῶνδε θερμὰ τέμνει καὶ διαιρεῖ καὶ διίστησι τὴν οὐσίαν, ὥστ’ ἐξ ἀνάγκης δακνώδη φαίνεσθαι, καθάπερ 625 καὶ τὸ ζέον ὕδωρ καὶ τὸ πῦρ αὐτό. καὶ τῶν ψυχρῶν δὲ κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον ὅσα μὲν συνάγει, οὔπω δὲ διασπᾷ τὸ συνεχὲς τοῦ πλησιάζοντος σώματος, ψυχρὰ μόνον ἐστὶν, οὐ μὴν ἤδη γέ πως δακνώδη. τὰ δὲ πρὸς τῷ συνάγειν διασπῶντα, βιαίως δὲ δηλονότι πάντα τὰ τοιαῦτα κέχρηται τῇ πιλήσει, δακνώδη φαντάζεται. τὸ μὲν οὖν συνάγειν τε καὶ πηγνύειν τὴν ὑποβεβλημένην ὕλην ἴδιον ἀεὶ τοῦ ψυχροῦ· τὸ δὲ διαχεῖν τε καὶ τήκειν τοῦ θερμοῦ, κοινὸν δ’ ἀμφοῖν ἀμετρότερα αὐξηθέντων τὸ δάκνειν. ὅσα μὲν οὖν ἀκριβῶς ἐστι ψυχρὰ καὶ θερμὰ, πρὸς τοῖς ἄλλοις ἅπασιν οἷς ἔμπροσθεν εἴπομεν ἀραιοῖς τε καὶ μαλακοῖς μέλεσιν, ἤδη καὶ τοῦ δέρματος ἡμῶν ἅπτεται. μέμνημαι δ’ ἐγώ ποτε διὰ χιόνος ὁδοιπορήσας οὕτω πολλῆς, ὡς μηδὲν τῆς γῆς φαίνεσθαι γυμνόν· ἦν δὲ καθαρὸς ὁ ἀὴρ ἀκριβῶς φαινόμενος καί τι πνεῦμα τῆς χιόνος ἀπέπνει, ψυχρότατον οὕτως, ὡς οὐ τοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς μόνους δάκνειν ἢ καὶ τοὺς μυκτῆρας, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὸ πρόσωπον ὅλον. εἰ δὲ καὶ χεῖρά τις ἔξω προὔβαλε, καὶ ταύτης ὁμοίως ἁπτόμενον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πιλήσει
But those that are still hotter than these cut and divide and separate the substance, so that of necessity they appear biting, just as 625 boiling water does and fire itself. And of cold things, in the same way, those that contract but do not yet tear apart the continuity of the body that comes near them are merely cold, but not yet in any way biting. But those that, in addition to contracting, tear apart — and plainly all such things employ compression violently — present themselves as biting. Now contracting and congealing the underlying matter is always the property of the cold, while diffusing and melting is that of the hot; but common to both, when they have been increased beyond measure, is biting. Those, then, that are precisely cold and hot, in addition to all the other rare and soft parts of which we spoke earlier, now take hold of our skin as well. And I myself remember once travelling through snow so deep that no part of the ground appeared bare; the air appeared perfectly clear, and a certain breath was blowing off from the snow, so extremely cold that it bit not only the eyes, or indeed the nostrils, but the whole face as well. And if someone put out a hand, it took hold of this too in the same way.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἁπτόμενον (participle with no finite verb; construed as 'it laid hold of'), ἀκριβῶς (in ὅσα μὲν οὖν ἀκριβῶς ἐστι ψυχρὰ καὶ θερμά, taken as 'in the strict sense'), ἅπτεται (taken as 'lay hold of')
But things still hotter than these cut, divide and separate the substance, so that of necessity they appear biting, as 625 do boiling water and fire itself. And in the same way, of cold things, those that draw together but do not yet tear apart the continuity of the body they come near are only cold, and not yet in any way biting. But those that tear apart in addition to drawing together appear biting; all such things evidently employ their compression violently. To draw together and to congeal the underlying matter is therefore always peculiar to the cold, and to diffuse and to melt is peculiar to the hot; but biting is common to both when they have increased beyond measure. Now those things that are cold and hot in the strict sense lay hold even of our skin, in addition to all the other rare and soft members of which we spoke earlier. I remember once journeying through snow so deep that no part of the ground appeared bare. The air appeared perfectly clear, and a breeze blew off the snow, so very cold that it bit not only the eyes, or the nostrils as well, but the whole face too. And if one also put a hand out, it laid hold of this likewise.

K11.625-626K. XI 625–626, ch. 4.2 difficulty mid (1.39/100; thin 2, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ὅσα δ’ ἀπολείπεται τῆς ἄκρας ψύξεως ἢ θερμότητος, οὐ δάκνει τὸ 626 δέρμα, τὰ μὲν ψύχοντα μήτε διασπᾶσθαί τι τῆς συνεχείας αὐτοῦ δυνάμενα διὰ τὴν σκληρότητα, μήτ’ εἰς τὸ βάθος ἐνδῦναι φθάνοντα τῇ πυκνώσει τῆς ἐπιφανείας· τὰ δὲ θερμαίνοντα τῷ τε μὴ διαβιβρώσκειν, ἀλλὰ διαχεῖν μόνον, τῷ τε διεξέρχεσθαι τοὺς πόρους ἐνίοτε ἑτοίμως. μέγιστον δ’ εἰς τὰ τοιαῦτα παθήματα συμβάλλεται τὸ τῆς οὐσίας τῶν φαρμάκων ἤτοι παχυμερὲς ἢ λεπτομερές. ἐπὶ μὲν γὰρ τῶν ψυχόντων τὰ λεπτομερῆ μᾶλλον ἐξικνεῖται πρὸς τὸ βάθος, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο δάκνει σφοδρότερον· ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν θερμαινόντων τὰ παχυμερῆ μᾶλλον ἑλκοῖ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἀνιᾷ βιαιότερον. λεπτομερέστερον μὲν οὖν ὁ ἀὴρ, παχυμερέστερον δὲ γῆ, μεταξὺ δ’ ἀμφοῖν τὸ ὕδωρ. ἀλλ’ ἀὴρ μὲν ἐκπυρωθεὶς φλὸξ γίγνεται, γῆ δὲ ἄνθραξ, τὸ δ’ ὕδωρ δέχεται μὲν ἰσχυρὰν θερμασίαν, ἀλλ’ οὔτε φλὸξ οὔτ’ ἄνθραξ γίνεται, διὰ τὴν σύμφυτον ὑγρότητα. φλὸξ μὲν γὰρ καὶ ἄνθραξ εἴδη πυρός.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But all those that fall short of extreme cooling or heat do not bite the 626 skin: the cooling ones, because they are able neither to tear apart anything of its continuity, on account of its hardness, nor to succeed in penetrating into the depth, because of the condensation of the surface; the heating ones, both because they do not corrode but only diffuse, and because they sometimes pass readily through the pores. And what contributes most to affections of this kind is the coarse-particled or fine-particled character of the substance of the drugs. For among the cooling ones the fine-particled reach the depth more, and for this reason bite more vehemently; while among the heating ones the coarse-particled ulcerate more, and for this reason cause distress more violently. Now air is more fine-particled, earth more coarse-particled, and water is between both. But air, when set on fire, becomes flame, and earth live coal, whereas water receives strong warmth but becomes neither flame nor live coal, because of its innate moisture. For flame and live coal are forms of fire.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φθάνοντα, διαχεῖν, διαβιβρώσκειν
But whatever falls short of extreme coldness or heat does not bite the 626 skin. The cooling ones are neither able to tear apart any of its continuity, on account of its hardness, nor do they succeed in penetrating into the depth, because of the condensation of the surface. The heating ones do not bite it because they do not eat through it but only diffuse, and because they sometimes pass readily through the pores. What contributes most to such affections is whether the substance of the drugs is coarse-particled or fine-particled. For among the cooling drugs the fine-particled reach further into the depth, and for this reason bite more vehemently; among the heating drugs the coarse-particled ulcerate more, and for this reason distress more violently. Now air is more fine-particled and earth more coarse-particled, and water lies between both. But air, when set on fire, becomes flame, and earth becomes live coal. Water admits a strong warmth, yet it becomes neither flame nor live coal, on account of its innate moisture. For flame and live coal are kinds of fire.

Greek (Kühn)
ἅπαν δὲ πῦρ ἐστιν θερμὸν καὶ ξηρὸν, οὐκ ἐγχωρεῖ δὲ τῷ ὕδατι γενέσθαι ξηρῷ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο, οὐ δέχεται τὴν τοῦ πυρὸς ἀκριβῶς ποιότητα, καθάπερ ἡ γῆ τε καὶ ὁ ἀήρ· φάρμακον μὲν οὖν 627 οὐδὲν οὕτω θερμόν ἐστιν ὡς φλὸξ ἢ ἄνθραξ ἢ ζέον ὕδωρ, ἀλλὰ κᾂν θερμότατον ᾖ, πάντως ποῦ γοῦν τούτων ἀπολείπεται πάμπολυ. μέχρι μέντοι τοῦ καίειν ἡ θερμότης αὐτῶν προέρχεται, ὅταν ἐν οὐσίᾳ περιέχηται παχυμερεῖ. τὰ δ’ ἐν ταῖς λεπτομερέσιν ἐνίοτε μὲν οὐδὲ δάκνει τὴν ἀρχὴν, ἀλλὰ θερμαίνει μόνον. ἔστι δ’ ὅτε δακνῶδες μέν ἐστιν, οὐ μὴν ἤδη καὶ καίει. Μεμνῆσθαι δὲ χρὴ τῆς ἐννοίας τῶν λεπτομερῶν τε καὶ παχυμερῶν, ὡς τὰ μὲν εἰς λεπτὰ μόρια ταχέως διαλυόμενα λεπτομερῆ προσαγορεύομεν, ὅσα δ’ οὐ δύναται πάσχειν τοῦτο, παχυμερῆ. τὸ μὲν οὖν διαλῦον αὐτὰ τὸ κατὰ τὸ σῶμα τοῦ ζώου θερμόν ἐστιν, ὥσπερ ἂν ἑκάστοτε τύχῃ προσφερόμενα· τὸ δ’ εἰς λεπτὰ καὶ παχέα μόρια θραύεσθαι παρὰ τῆς ἑαυτῶν ἔχει φύσεως, ὥστε καὶ τῶν ψυχόντων φαρμάκων ὅσα μέν ἐστι παχυμερῆ δάκνειν οὐ δύναται, μὴ φθάνοντά γε διαδύεσθαι τὴν ἔξωθεν τοῦ δέρματος ἐπιφάνειαν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πάντως ποῦ γοῦν
And all fire is hot and dry, but it is not possible for water to become dry, and for this reason it does not receive the quality of fire exactly, as earth and air do. No drug, then, 627 is as hot as flame or live coal or boiling water; but even if it is extremely hot, it certainly, at any rate, falls very far short of these. Nevertheless their heat does advance as far as burning, whenever it is contained in a coarse-particled substance. But those [heats] contained in fine-particled substances sometimes do not bite at all, but only heat; and there are times when it is biting, yet does not thereby also burn. One must keep in mind the notion of fine-particled and coarse-particled things, namely that we call fine-particled those which are quickly dissolved into fine parts, and coarse-particled those which cannot undergo this. Now that which dissolves them is the heat in the body of the animal, in whatever manner they happen to be applied on each occasion; but their being broken up into fine or coarse parts they have from their own nature, so that, among cooling drugs too, those which are coarse-particled cannot bite, since they do not get so far as to penetrate the outer surface of the skin.

All fire is hot and dry, but it is not possible for water to become dry, and for this reason it does not receive the quality of fire precisely, as earth and air do. Now no drug 627 is as hot as flame or live coal or boiling water; even if it is very hot, it surely falls very far short of these at any rate. Their heat does, however, advance as far as burning when it is contained in a coarse-particled substance. Those whose heat is in fine-particled substances sometimes do not bite at all, but only heat. At other times the drug is biting, but does not yet also burn. One must remember the conception of fine-particled and coarse-particled things: those that are quickly dissolved into fine parts we call fine-particled, and those that cannot undergo this we call coarse-particled. What dissolves them is the heat in the body of the animal, in whatever way they happen to be applied on each occasion. Their being broken up into fine or coarse parts, however, they have from their own nature. So also among the cooling drugs, those that are coarse-particled cannot bite, since they do not succeed in getting through the outer surface of the skin.

But those that are fine-particled both penetrate easily and reach to the 628 depth and bite the soft parts of the animal; and still more biting are those that are at once fine-particled and uneven in their mixtures. And it has been shown in what precedes that nearly all are uneven. For it is either impossible or very difficult to find any substance whatsoever that is precisely homoeomerous. At any rate, we said that wine has in itself one coarse-particled residue, from which, as it is separated out and carried down over time, the lees are formed; and another, which is called the 'flower', which floats on the surface during the time of boiling, but later is carried down into the lees; and a third, watery one, mixed through the whole of the wine, to which also the boiling belongs first and foremost. And a fourth, in addition to these, is the real wine itself. And it has been shown that in oil too and in milk and in all the other juices there is a similar nature of residues, and still more in all mined substances taken as wholes.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐμπύρευμα
Having recalled these things, we must now investigate concerning all drugs, and first of all guard against suffering what most of those who have inquired into such matters 629 have suffered. For they construct their argument as though all of them were homoeomerous, not conceding that either wine or oil or any of the others is composed of parts differing in their powers; and still more than in these cases, with regard to vinegar they are amazed if we dare to say that it has lost the innate heat of the wine but possesses the heat that comes from putrefaction—which is indeed also the view of Aristotle and Theophrastus. For the vinous parts of the wine are chilled in the change into vinegar, while the watery residue, as it putrefies, comes to possess a certain acquired heat, just as do all other things that have putrefied. And vinegar becomes something composite, made up of parts most opposite in their powers, some of them chilled, others hot, just like all the ashes of burnt wood. For in those too what is, as it were, an ember-like residue (empyreuma) is scattered about in small particles, and this is quite hot, while all the rest is earthy and cold.

Having recalled these things, we must now examine all drugs, and first guard against suffering what most of those who have investigated such matters 629 have suffered. For they frame their argument as though all things were homoeomerous, not granting that either wine or oil or any of the other things is composed of parts differing in their powers; and still more than at these, in the case of vinegar they are astonished if we dare to say of it that it has lost the innate heat of the wine and has instead heat from putrefaction, which is indeed the view of both Aristotle and Theophrastus. For the vinous parts of the wine are cooled off in the change into vinegar, while the watery residue, putrefying, takes on a certain acquired heat, just as all other things that have putrefied do. And so vinegar becomes something compounded of parts most opposite in their powers, some cooled off, others hot, as are all the ashes of burnt wood. For in these too a sort of ember is scattered about in small particles, and this is quite hot, while all the rest is earthy and cold.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And for this reason, whenever ash that has been steeped in water is strained in due measure through certain rare bodies, the hot and 630 pungent parts are carried off together with it in this process, while what is left over is no longer hot, since it has deposited its fiery parts in the water. People call such water lye, which is analogous to sea-water and brine in respect of its condition, its generation, and its power. For those are composed of water and salts, while the water that washes through the ash, having become composite out of itself and the parts it has carried along, in this way produces what is named lye; and if we did not already know beforehand that it came into being from water and the sooty parts of the ash, we would perhaps suppose it to be a simple and uncompounded body.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συμμέτρως, διαθέσεως, ἐπηνέγκατο
And for this reason, whenever ash moistened with water is strained in due measure through certain rare bodies, the hot and 630 pungent parts are carried off along with it in this process, while what remains is no longer hot, having deposited its fiery parts in the water. People call such water lye (κονία), analogous to sea-water and brine in respect of disposition, of generation and of power. For those are composed of water and salt, whereas the water that washes round the ash, having become composite out of itself and of the parts it carried along with it, has in this way produced what is named lye. Had we not known beforehand that this lye came into being out of water and the sooty parts of the ash, we might perhaps have supposed it to be a simple and uncompounded body.

τοιοῦτον γάρ τι κᾀπὶ τοῦ ὄξους πεπόνθαμεν, οὐ δυνάμενοι δι’ αἰσθήσεως ἰδεῖν αὐτοῦ τὴν γένεσιν, ἀπιστοῦμεν τῷ λόγῳ· ἀλλ’ εἰ μηδὲν ἄλλο, τὴν γοῦν τῆς ὄμφακος διαφορὰν πρὸς αὐτὸ κατανοήσαντες δίκαιοι πιστεύειν ἐσόμεθα ὡς οὐκ ὀξὺς μόνον, ἀλλὰ δριμύς τις ἕτερος ἀναμέμικται χυμὸς αὐτῷ, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τελέως ὁ τῆς ὄμφακος ἐμψύχει χυλὸς, ὥστε καὶ πρὸς καῦσον ἄκρως ἁρμόττει, κατὰ τοῦ στόματος τῆς γαστρὸς ἢ τῶν ὑποχονδρίων ὅλων ἐπιτιθέμενος, καὶ πρὸς ἅπαν ὅ τι περ ἂν ἐμψύξαι βουληθῶμεν, 631 οὐχ ὁμοίως δ’ ὄξος. οὐ γὰρ ἀκριβῶς ἓν οὐδ’ ὀξὺ μόνον ἐστὶν, ἀλλὰ καὶ δριμύ. τὸ μὲν οὖν ὀξὺ μόνον ὅ τι περ ἂν ᾖ ψυχρὸν πάντως ἐστὶν εἴτ’ οὖν ἄπιος, εἴτε μῆλον, εἴτε ῥοῦς, εἴτε ὁ ἀπὸ τῶν βάτων καρπὸς, εἴτε ὁ ἀπὸ τῆς μορέας, εἴθ’ ὁ ἀπὸ τῆς ῥοιᾶς, εἴθ’ ὅστις οὖν ἄλλος ἢ καρπὸς, ἢ χυλὸς, ἢ φυτὸν, ὡς ὀξυλάπαθον καὶ ὀξαλὶς, ἣν δὴ καὶ ὀξύδα προσαγορεύουσιν. ἂν γάρ σοι γευομένῳ σφοδρὰ μὲν καὶ σαφὴς ἡ ὀξύτης ἐν αὐτῷ καταφαίνηται, μηδὲν δ’ ἐμφαίνηται δριμύτητος, πάντως τοῦτον εὑρήσεις ἐμψύχοντα τὸν χυμὸν, καὶ χρῶ θαῤῥῶν, εἴτε πρὸς καῦσον ἐθέλοις χρῆσθαι καθ’ ὑποχονδρίων ἐπιτιθεὶς, εἴτε πρὸς ἐρυσίπελας, εἴτε πρὸς ἄλλο τι πάθος θερμόν. ὡσαύτως δὲ καὶ τὰ ψυχρὰ πάντα νοσήματα βλαπτόμενα πρὸς αὐτοῦ σαφῶς εὑρήσεις. ὅταν δ’ ἐπίμηκτος ἡ ποιότης φαίνηταί σοι, μετὰ μὲν δριμύτητος καὶ ὑποτοπεῖσθαι χρὴ θερμότητα, μετὰ δὲ στύψεως ἕτερόν τι γένος ψυχρότητος. ἡ μὲν γὰρ ὀξεῖα ψυχρότης λεπτομερὴς, ἡ δὲ στύφουσα παχυμερής.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ῥοῦς, ὀξύδα, χυμὸς
For we have experienced something of this sort also in the case of vinegar: being unable to see its generation through sense-perception, we disbelieve the argument. But, if nothing else, having at least observed the difference of the unripe grape from it, we shall be right to believe that not only a sour flavour but also some other, pungent flavour has been mixed into it; and for this reason the juice of the unripe grape cools completely, so that it is also supremely suited for causus, when applied over the mouth of the stomach or the whole of the hypochondria, and for everything whatsoever that we may wish to cool, 631 whereas vinegar is not so in like manner. For it is not strictly one thing, nor sour only, but also pungent. So then, whatever is sour only is in every case cold, whether it be pear, or apple, or sumach, or the fruit from brambles, or that from the mulberry, or that from the pomegranate, or whatever other fruit, or juice, or plant, such as oxylapathon and oxalis, which indeed they also call oxys. For if, when you taste it, the sourness in it appears to you strong and clear, but nothing of pungency shows itself, you will in every case find this flavour to be cooling; and use it with confidence, whether you wish to use it against causus, applying it on the hypochondria, or against erysipelas, or against some other hot affection. And in the same way you will clearly find all cold diseases harmed by it. But when the quality appears to you mixed, then along with pungency one must also suspect heat, while along with astringency one must suspect another kind of coldness. For sour coldness is fine-particled, whereas astringent coldness is coarse-particled.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀξύδα, ἐπίμηκτος, ῥοῦς, χυμός
For we have experienced something of this sort in the case of vinegar too: being unable to see its generation through sense-perception, we disbelieve the argument. But, if nothing else, once we have considered at least its difference from the unripe grape, we shall be right to believe that not a sour flavour only, but also some other, pungent flavour is mixed into it. For this reason the juice of the unripe grape cools completely, so that it is also supremely suited to causus, when applied over the mouth of the stomach or over the whole of the hypochondria, and to anything whatever that we may wish to cool, 631 but vinegar does not do so in like manner. For it is not strictly one thing, nor sour only, but also pungent. Now whatever is sour only is in every case cold, whether a pear, or an apple, or sumach, or the fruit from brambles, or that from the mulberry, or that from the pomegranate, or any other whatever, be it fruit, or juice, or plant, such as oxylapathon and oxalis, which they also call oxys. For if, when you taste it, the sourness in it shows itself strong and distinct, while nothing of pungency appears in it, you will find this flavour cooling in every case, and you may use it with confidence, whether you wish to use it for causus, applying it over the hypochondria, or for erysipelas, or for any other hot affection. In the same way you will also clearly find all the cold diseases harmed by it. But when the quality appears to you mixed, then with pungency one must also suspect heat, and with astringency some other kind of coldness. For sour coldness is fine-particled, astringent coldness coarse-particled.

Greek (Kühn)
632 Ἀλλὰ τούτων μὲν ὀλίγον ὕστερον ἐροῦμεν τὰς πίστεις. ὅ δὲ χρὴ πρότερον συμβουλεῦσαι τοῖς τὰ τοιαῦτα χωρὶς ἀλαζονείας σοφιστικῆς αὐτῆς ἕνεκεν τῆς ἀληθείας ἐπισκοπουμένοις, ἤδη μοι καιρὸς εἰπεῖν, ὡς οὐ χρὴ μόνῳ τῷ λόγῳ γεγυμνάσθαι δι’ ὧν ἀεὶ παρακελεύομαι μεθόδων λογικῶν, ἃς ἐν τοῖς περὶ τῶν ἀποδείξεων ὑπομνήμασιν εἶπον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὴν αἴσθησιν τὴν γευστικὴν ἐπὶ τῶν χυμῶν γυμνάζειν ἐπιμελῶς, ἀρχομένους ἀπὸ τῶν ἐναργεστάτην ἐχόντων ἡντιναοῦν ποιότητα μίαν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χυμῶν
632 But the proofs of these matters we shall state a little later. What it is necessary first to advise those who investigate such things, without sophistical imposture, for the sake of truth itself, it is now time for me to say: that one must not only be trained in reasoning, by means of the logical methods which I constantly recommend, and which I set out in my commentaries on demonstrations, but must also carefully exercise the sense of taste upon the humours (flavours), beginning from those that possess some single quality whatsoever in the most evident manner.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
632 But we shall state the proofs of these things a little later. What must first be advised to those who examine such matters without sophistical imposture, for the sake of the truth itself, it is now time for me to say: that one must not be trained in reason alone, through the logical methods which I am always urging and which I set out in the commentaries On Demonstration, but must also diligently train the gustatory sense on flavours, beginning from those that possess one single quality, whichever it may be, in the most evident form.

K11.632-633K. XI 632–633, ch. 4.4 difficulty high (3.09/100; thin 3, absent 3) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 14/14 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
εἰ μὲν τὴν δριμύτητα κατανοῆσαι σαφῶς ἐθέλοις, ἐπὶ σκορόδου καὶ κρομμύου καὶ τῶν ὁμοίων αὐτοῖς, ἀπογευόμενός τε συνεχῶς καὶ διαμασώμενον ἐπὶ πλεῖστον καὶ τὴν μνήμην πειρώμενον ἀκριβῶς παρατίθεσθαι τοῦ γιγνομένου πάθους τὴν αἴσθησιν· εἰ δὲ τὴν στύψιν, ἐπὶ κικίδος τε καὶ ῥοῦ καὶ τῶν ὁμοίων· εἰ δὲ τὴν πικρότητα, νίτρου καὶ χολῆς γευόμενον· εἰ δὲ τὴν γλυκύτητα, σιραίου καὶ μέλιτος, ἔτι δὲ πρὸς τούτοις εἴτ’ οὖν ἄποιον ἐθέλοις εἴτε μέσον ἐν ποιότητι τῇ πρὸς τὴν γεῦσιν ὑπολαμβάνειν, τὸ 633 ὕδωρ ἀξιῶ σε καὶ τούτου γευσάμενον, ἀκριβῶς ἔχειν ἐν τῇ μνήμῃ, καὶ μάλιστά γε τοῦ μηδεμίαν ὧν νῦν εἴρηκα ἐμφαίνοντος ποιότητα, μήτε γλυκύτητα μήτε δριμύτητα μήτ’ ὀξύτητα μήτε πικρότητα, καὶ πρὸς τούτοις ἔτι μήθ’ ἱκανῶς θερμοῦ μήθ’ ὑπερβαλλόντως ψυχροῦ. ἐντεῦθεν γὰρ ὁρμώμενος ἑτοιμότερον ἐπί τε τὴν τῶν ἄλλων ἁπάντων ἀμυδρῶν χυμῶν ἀφίξῃ διάγνωσιν ἐπί τε τὴν τῶν ὡς ἐγὼ μέν φημι γλυκέων, ὡς δ’ ἄλλοι τινὲς, ὑδατωδῶν, οἷος ὅ τε τῶν καλάμων ἐστὶ τῶν χλωρῶν καὶ ἀγρώστεως καὶ προσέτι πυρῶν καὶ κριθῶν καὶ ζειῶν καὶ ἄλλων πολλῶν, ὧν καὶ Θεόφραστος μέμνηται, ζητῶν εἴτ’ εἴδη πλείονα θετέον ἐστὶν τῶν γλυκέων χυμῶν, εἴτε τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον αὐτοὺς ὑποληπτέον διαφέρειν ἀλλήλων. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τούτων αὖθις διαιρήσομαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπογευόμενός τε συνεχῶς καὶ διαμασώμενον ἐπὶ πλεῖστον καὶ τὴν μνήμην πειρώμενον (case inconsistency nominative/accusative), ἀγρώστεως
If you wish to understand pungency clearly, [do so] in the case of garlic and onion and the things similar to them, tasting continually and chewing for a very long time and trying to store accurately in your memory the perception of the affection that arises; if astringency, in the case of gall-nut and sumach and the like; if bitterness, by tasting nitre and bile; if sweetness, boiled-down must and honey; and further, in addition to these, whether you wish to take water as being without quality or as intermediate in quality with respect to taste, I ask you, having tasted this 633 water too, to hold it accurately in memory, and most of all that water which displays none of the qualities I have now mentioned, neither sweetness nor pungency nor sourness nor bitterness, and in addition to these, moreover, which is neither considerably hot nor excessively cold. For setting out from here you will arrive more readily both at the discrimination of all the other faint flavours and at that of those which, as I for my part say, are sweet, but as certain others say, watery, such as is the flavour of green reeds and of dog's-tooth grass and, in addition, of wheat and barley and emmer and many others, which Theophrastus too mentions, inquiring whether several species of sweet flavours are to be posited, or whether they are to be supposed to differ from one another by the more and the less. But concerning these matters I shall make the distinctions again later.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀγρώστεως, ζειῶν, ῥοῦ, ἀμυδρῶν
If you wish to apprehend pungency clearly, do so with garlic and onion and things similar to them, tasting them repeatedly and chewing them for as long as possible, and trying to lay up accurately in your memory the sensation of the affection that arises; if astringency, with oak-gall and sumach and things similar; if bitterness, by tasting nitre and bile; if sweetness, boiled-down must and honey. And further, in addition to these, whether you wish to regard it as without quality or as intermediate in quality with respect to taste, I ask that you taste 633 water too and hold it accurately in your memory, and above all water that displays none of the qualities I have just named, neither sweetness nor pungency nor sourness nor bitterness, and in addition to these is neither notably hot nor excessively cold. For setting out from this you will arrive more readily at the discernment of all the other faint flavours, and at that of those which I call sweet but certain others call watery, such as the flavour of green reeds and of agrostis-grass, and moreover of wheat and barley and zeia and many others, which Theophrastus also mentions when he inquires whether several species of sweet flavours are to be posited, or whether they are to be supposed to differ from one another by the more and the less. But concerning these I shall draw distinctions later.

    If you wish to understand pungency clearly, [do so] in the case of garlic and onion and the things similar to them, tasting continually and chewing for a very long time and trying to store accurately in your memory the perception of the affection that arises; if astringency, in the case of gall-nut and sumach and the like; if bitterness, by tasting nitre and bile; if sweetness, boiled-down must and honey; and further, in addition to these, whether you wish to take water as being without quality or as intermediate in quality with respect to taste, I ask you, having tasted this [633] water too, to hold it accurately in memory, and most of all that water which displays none of the qualities I have now mentioned, neither sweetness nor pungency nor sourness nor bitterness, and in addition to these, moreover, which is neither considerably hot nor excessively cold.

    If you wish to apprehend pungency clearly, do so with garlic and onion and things similar to them, tasting them repeatedly and chewing them for as long as possible, and trying to lay up accurately in your memory the sensation of the affection that arises; if astringency, with oak-gall and sumach and things similar; if bitterness, by tasting nitre and bile; if sweetness, boiled-down must and honey. Greek (Kühn)
Ὁποίαν δ’ ἄν σοι τὸ ἀποιότατον ὕδωρ αἴσθησιν παράσχῃ, τοιαύτην ὅταν ἔν τινι τῶν ξηρῶν εὑρίσκῃς, ἔξω δηλονότι πάσης τὸ τοιοῦτον θερμότητός τε καὶ ψυχρότητος ἐπιφανοῦς ἐστι, μέσην ὡς οἷόν τε μάλιστα κατάστασιν ﻿ 634 ἔχον, ἢ καὶ μικρόν τι ῥέπον ἐπὶ τὸ ψυχρόν. ἀλλ’ εἴπερ οὕτως ἔχον ἐν τῇ κατὰ θερμότητα καὶ ψυχρότητα διαφορᾷ ξηρὸν εἴη τὴν σύστασιν, ἀνάγκη γεῶδες ὑπάρχειν αὐτὸ καὶ ξηραίνειν ἀδήκτως. ὀνομάζεται δὲ πρὸς τῶν ἰατρῶν ἅπαντα τὰ τοιαῦτα τῶν φαρμάκων ἐμπλαστικὰ, καθάπερ τὰ πλεῖστα τῶν ἀκριβῶς πεπλυμένων, ἄμυλος, πομφόλυξ καὶ ψιμμύθιον καὶ τίτανος καὶ καδμεία καὶ γῆ ἡ Κρητικὴ καὶ Κιμωλία καὶ χόνδρος καὶ Σάμιος ἀστὴρ καὶ ἡ τῶν γναφέων γῆ, τῶν ἐμπλαστικῶν ἐστι φαρμάκων. τινὰ δ’ ἐξ αὐτῶν οὐ γεώδη μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὑδατώδη τὴν φύσιν ἐστὶν, ἔνια δὲ καὶ ἀέρα οὐκ ὀλίγον ἐν αὐτοῖς περιέχει, γλίσχρα μέντοι ταῦτα πάντα καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἐμπλαστικά. Διττὴ γὰρ ἡ τῶν ἐμπλαστικῶν ἐστι φύσις, ἡ μὲν ἑτέρα γεώδης ἀκριβῶς καὶ ξηρὰ, ἡ δ’ ἑτέρα γλίσχρα μὲν πάντως, μικτὴ δ’ ἐξ ὕδατος καὶ γῆς καὶ τὰ πολλὰ καὶ ἀέρος, ὥσπερ καὶ τοὔλαιον τὸ γλυκύ. λέλεκται δ’ ἔμπροσθεν ὁποῖον λέγω τὸ γλυκύ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χόνδρος
Whatever sensation the most qualityless water affords you, whenever you find such a sensation in one of the dry things, such a thing is clearly outside all manifest heat and cold, having a condition 634 as nearly as possible in the middle, or even inclining a little toward the cold. But if, being so disposed in respect of the difference according to heat and cold, it should be dry in its consistency, it must necessarily be earthy and dry without biting. All such drugs are called by the physicians "clogging", just as most of those that have been thoroughly washed; starch, pompholyx and white lead and lime and cadmia and Cretan earth and Cimolian earth and chondros and Samian star and fullers' earth are among the clogging drugs. Some of them are not only earthy but also watery in nature, and some also contain in themselves not a little air; all these, however, are viscous, and for this reason clogging. For the nature of the clogging drugs is twofold: the one precisely earthy and dry, the other in every case viscous, but mixed from water and earth and for the most part also air, as sweet oil also is. It has been said earlier what kind of thing I mean by "sweet".
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τίτανος, χόνδρος, Σάμιος ἀστὴρ, ἀποιότατον
Whatever sensation the water that is most devoid of quality affords you, when you find such a sensation in one of the dry substances, such a substance is evidently outside all manifest heat and coldness, having a state as nearly intermediate as possible 634, or else one inclining slightly towards the cold. But if, being so disposed as regards the difference of heat and coldness, it is dry in consistency, it must necessarily be earthy and dry without biting. All such drugs are called by the physicians clogging, as are most of those that have been thoroughly washed: starch, pompholyx, white lead, lime, cadmia, Cretan earth, Cimolian earth, groats, Samian star and fullers' earth belong to the clogging drugs. Some of them are not only earthy but also watery in nature, and some even contain no small amount of air within them; all these, however, are viscous, and for this reason clogging. For the nature of clogging substances is twofold: the one exactly earthy and dry, the other wholly viscous, but mixed of water and earth and in most cases also of air, as is sweet olive oil too. I have said earlier what I mean by sweet.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τὴν τῶν ὑῶν, ἔτι γὰρ
This, then, is mixed from airy and earthy and watery substance together; the white of the egg 635 is composed in some way from the same things, but is earthier than oil; and indeed the cheesy part of milk is clogging too, as is the soft fat of the pig. For the soft fat of a bull and of a he-goat has much pungency, and fiery pungency at that; that of an ox and a she-goat less so, yet the hard fat of these too is already somewhat pungent. For indeed the soft fat of such animals is called hard fat (stear), on account of its thickness; for in addition it is not only hotter but also earthier than that of pigs. That of a goose and of a cock is both hotter and drier than that of pigs, but it is also fine-particled and least earthy. So too is that of all winged creatures, because the substance of their body too has no small share of the airy. But about these there is another discussion. At any rate, all soft fats that do not yet have any pungency are clogging of the pores, and the drier and earthier ones more so. Such too is washed wax; for it too lays aside the pungency that comes from the honey. It is evident that these things are not said about wax that is bitter by nature, as the Pontic is.

ἐῥ ἀψινθίου 636 γὰρ ἐκεῖνος ἔχει τὴν πικρότητα, καὶ εἰ πλύναις αὐτὸν μυριάκις, οὐκ ἂν ἐκνίψαις τελέως τὴν πικρότητα. φυλάττεσθαι δὲ καὶ τὸν ἐκ δριμέος καὶ θυμώδους μέλιτος κηρὸν, ὅταν ἄδηκτον ἀκριβῶς ἐθέλοις σκευάσαι φάρμακον. ἐπιτήδειος οὖν ὁ ἐξ ὑδατωδεστέρου καὶ ἥκιστα δριμέος μέλιτος εἰς τὸ πλύνεσθαι. For that one has its bitterness from wormwood, and even if you were to wash it ten thousand times, you would not wash the bitterness out completely. One must also beware of the wax derived from pungent and thyme-flavoured honey, whenever you wish to prepare a drug that is entirely non-biting. Suitable for washing, therefore, is the wax from a more watery and least pungent honey. Such, then, are the clogging drugs. Concerning the astringent and the sour ones I return once more. The astringent ones are earthy and coarse-particled in the consistency of their masses, and cold in their quality; the sour ones are fine-particled in their bodies, but cold in the same way as the astringent ones. The proof concerning both is twofold: according to sense-perception, by way of inference (ἐξ ἀπαγωγῆς), and according to reason, from their substance. Of the proofs there will be stated first the one that is clearer to most people, but weaker with regard to scientific demonstration.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τῶν ὑποκειμένων ἡμῖν ἀεὶ στοιχείων
All the fruits from trees, as many as appear sweet to us when they have ripened, are, when just coming into being, both acerbic and dry in their consistency, each according to the nature of the 637 tree that generates it, as Theophrastus too said somewhere—olives, grapes, apples, pomegranates, mulberries, dates, pears. But as they advance in time they become moister, and they take on sourness in addition to the acerbity, and, laying this aside little by little, they become sweet in turn as they are brought to completion. Some, however, do not acquire the sweetness even on the trees, but only after they have been removed, with time. Others again do not pass through sourness as an intermediate stage, but arrive directly from acerbity at sweetness, as does also that of the olive. And indeed, that all are concocted by the hot is clear, and that this is of two kinds—the one the proper innate heat of each, the other from without, from the sun, yet connate and proper to them—we surely know plainly, if we remember the elements that always underlie our account. But if they are more acerbic at the beginning, and become sweet as they ripen, then their sweetness arose for them from warmth, and their sourness and acerbity from cooling.

All the fruits of trees which appear sweet to us when ripened are, when just being formed, acerbic and dry in consistency, each in accordance with the nature of the 637 tree that produces it, as Theophrastus too said somewhere: olives, grapes, apples, pomegranates, mulberries, dates, pears. But if they are more acerbic at the beginning and become sweet as they ripen, then their sweetness has come about from warmth, and their sourness and acerbity from cooling.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κατατηκόμενα
And since, at 638 the beginning, while they are still woody, as Theophrastus too said, they are at once dry and acerbic, whereas the abundance of the moisture flowing into them renders them sour, it is evident that the acerbic flavour is earthier, the sour one moister; so that Plato too spoke correctly when he defined them: 'all such earthy particles as are carried in the juices, which, being dissolved (κατατηκόμενα) and contracting and drying up the moist portions of the sensitive parts of the tongue, appear acerbic when they are rougher, and harsh when they roughen less.' And we, in addition to this, shall correctly say that such a flavour is also cold in its mixture. For if it were earthy only, it would be clogging, not acerbic; and in a way the argument, having now passed over to the very substance of the flavours, lays hold of scientific demonstration.

Since, moreover, 638 at the beginning, while still woody, as Theophrastus also said, they are at once dry and acerbic, and it is the quantity of the moisture flowing in upon them that makes them sour, it is evident that the acerbic flavour is more earthy and the sour one moister. Hence Plato too, in defining them, rightly said: all such earthy particles as are carried along in the juices, melting down and contracting and drying out the moist parts of the sensory parts of the tongue, appear acerbic when they are rougher, and harsh when they roughen less. And we in addition to these points shall rightly say that such a flavour is also cold in its mixture. For if it were only earthy, it would be clogging, not acerbic; and in a way the argument, having now passed over to the very substance of the flavours, lays hold of scientific demonstration.

For if it clearly belongs to the acerbic juice to contract and dry and roughen the tongue, and it was shown in the preceding discussions that bodies are contracted and constricted and compacted by cold alone, it is evident that the acerbic flavour is in every case cold; and since it also dries unevenly 639 — for this was what does the roughening — it would in every case also be earthy. For the watery [flavour] passes through everything evenly. For it is inseparable from it, even if it is torn apart, to be united again; whereas for the earthy body that is carried along in the juices both being torn apart is already present, and no longer coming together easily belongs to it as well. And moreover, the peculiar character of the sensation of each of the two affections, if you recall it, will bear witness to the same things. For the passage of sour flavours through the sentient bodies appears to take place quickly, but that of acerbic ones slowly. And sour things appear to act rather in depth, acerbic things on the surface. All these things, then, indicate that acerbic bodies are coarse-particled, while sour ones are fine-particled.

For if the acerbic juice clearly has the property of contracting, drying and roughening the tongue, and it was shown in the preceding discussions that bodies are contracted, constricted and compacted by cold alone, it is evident that the acerbic flavour is altogether cold. And sour things are seen to act more in depth, acerbic things on the surface. All these things, then, indicate that acerbic bodies are coarse-particled and that sour ones are fine-particled.

ταυτὶ μὲν οὖν ἱκανῶς ἀλλήλοις ὡμολόγηται, τά τ’ ἀπὸ τῆς οὐσίας αὐτῆς τῶν πραγμάτων εἰρημένα καὶ τὰ διὰ τῆς τῶν εὑρημένων κατὰ τὴν αἴσθησιν ἐπαγωγῆς· οὐδὲν δ’ ἂν εἴη χεῖρον ἔτι καὶ ταῦτα προσθεῖναι, μεμνημένους πάλιν κᾀνταῦθα τῆς τῶν ὀνομάτων χρήσεως, ὡς στύφειν μὲν ἄμφω λέγεται, τό τ’ αὐστηρὸν καὶ τὸ στρυφνὸν, ἐν δὲ τῷ μᾶλλον 640 καὶ ἧττον διαφέρει. καὶ μὴν καὶ ὅτι πολλαὶ τοῖς φαρμάκοις ὑπάρχουσι ποιότητες οὐ διάφοροι μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ μαχόμεναι πρὸς ἀλλήλας, ἐνίοτε δὲ καὶ παντάπασιν ὑπεναντίαι, δέδεικται μὲν ἔμπροσθεν αὐτάρκως, ἄρξασθαι δ’ ἀπ’ αὐτοῦ καὶ νῦν ἀναγκαῖον. εἰ μὲν γάρ σοι φαίνοιτο γευομένῳ καὶ στρυφνὸν ἅμα καὶ δάκνον ταὐτὸ, τουτὶ μὲν ἀπολιπεῖν σε κελεύσω τὸ φάρμακον, ἔρχεσθαι δ’ ἐπὶ τὸ στρυφνὸν ἄνευ τοῦ δάκνειν. εἰ δὲ μηδ’ ὀξὺ φαίνοιτο, μηδὲ γλυκὺ, μηδὲ πικρὸν, ἀλλ’ ὡς οἷόν τε μάλιστα μίαν ἔχον ἄμικτον ποιότητα τὴν τῆς στύψεως, οὕτως ἤδη βασανίζειν αὐτὸ τῇ πείρᾳ καθ’ ὃν πολλάκις ἔμπροσθεν ὑπεθέμην σοι τρόπον. ἐὰν γὰρ ἅμα τό τε στύφειν ἔχῃ καὶ τὸ δάκνειν ἤ τινα ἄλλην ἐναργῆ ποιότητα καὶ δύναμιν ἐπιμεμιγμένην τῇ στύψει, περιττὸν καὶ μάταιον εἰς πεῖραν ἄγειν τὸ τοιοῦτον φάρμακον, ἐπειδὰν δοκιμάζῃς ἐνέργειαν στύψεως. ἄδηλον γὰρ εἴτε διὰ τὴν στύψιν, εἴτε διά τινα τῶν ἀναμεμιγμένων αὐτῇ ποιοτήτων, εἴτε καὶ δι’ ἀμφοτέρας ἐνήργησεν ὡδί πως περὶ τὸ πλησιάζον αὐτῷ σῶμα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
These points, then, are sufficiently in agreement with one another — both what has been said on the basis of the very substance of the things and what has been said by way of induction from what is discovered by perception. But it would be no worse to add these points too, recalling here again the usage of the names: that both are said to be astringent, the harsh and the acerbic, but they differ in the more 640 and the less. And indeed, that drugs possess many qualities, not only different but also in conflict with one another, and sometimes even wholly opposed, has been sufficiently shown before; but it is necessary now too to begin from this point. For if, when you taste it, the same thing appears to you to be at once both acerbic and biting, I shall bid you leave this drug aside and go on to the one that is acerbic without being biting. And if it appears neither sour nor sweet nor bitter, but, as far as is at all possible, having a single unmixed quality, that of astringency, then at that point to test it by experience in the manner I have often laid down for you before. For if it has at once both the astringent and the biting, or some other evident quality and power mingled with the astringency, it is superfluous and vain to bring such a drug to the test when you are examining the activity of astringency. For it is unclear whether it was because of the astringency, or because of one of the qualities mixed in with it, or indeed because of both, that it acted in this or that way upon the body that came into contact with it.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡδί πως, ὑπεναντίαι
These things, then, agree sufficiently with one another: both what has been said on the basis of the very substance of the things, and what has been said through induction from what has been found by perception. But it would be no worse to add the following as well, remembering here too, once again, the usage of the names: both the harsh and the acerbic are said to be astringent, but they differ in the more 640 and the less. Moreover, that drugs possess many qualities, not only different but also conflicting with one another, and sometimes even wholly contrary, has been shown sufficiently earlier; but it is necessary to begin from this point now as well. For if on tasting it the same thing should appear to you at once acerbic and biting, I shall bid you leave this drug aside and go on to the acerbic one that is without biting. And if it should appear neither sour, nor sweet, nor bitter, but, as far as is possible, to have one unmixed quality, that of astringency, then you should at once test it by trial in the manner I have often proposed to you before. For if it has at the same time both being astringent and biting, or some other evident quality and power mixed in with the astringency, it is superfluous and vain to bring such a drug to trial when you are assessing the action of astringency. For it is unclear whether it acted in this particular way upon the body near it because of the astringency, or because of one of the qualities mixed in with it, or because of both.

Greek (Kühn)
χαλκῖτις οὖν καὶ μίσυ 641 καὶ χάλκανθος καὶ λεπὶς χαλκοῦ καὶ σῶρυ, στύφοντά τε ἅμα καὶ δάκνοντα, δρᾷ μὲν κατ’ ἀμφοτέρας τὰς ποιότητας εἰς ἕκαστον τῶν ὁμιλούντων σωμάτων, οὐ μὴν διδάξαι γε ἡμᾶς δύναται σαφῶς εἴτε διὰ τὴν στύψιν ἔχει τὸ καίειν, εἴτε διὰ τὴν δριμύτητα. καταλιπεῖν οὖν ἄμεινον αὐτὰ καὶ γεύεσθαι πολλῶν ἐφεξῆς ἄλλων ἐπισκοπούμενον εἰλικρινῆ στύψιν ἰδίᾳ καὶ καθ’ ἑαυτὴν ὡς οἷόν τ’ ἐστὶ μάλιστα, κᾀπειδὰν τοιαύτην εὕρῃς, οὕτως ἤδη κρίνειν τὸ φάρμακον αἷς πολλάκις ἔμπροσθεν ἤκουσας μεθόδοις, οἷον εἴ σοι γευομένῳ φαίνοιτο βαλαύστιον, ἢ κικὶς, ἢ κύτινος, ἢ ὑποκυστὶς, ἢ στρύχνος, ἢ γλαύκιον, ἢ ἀκακία, ἢ ῥοῦ χυλὸς, ἤ τι τοιοῦτον ἕτερον ἀκριβῶς μὲν εἶναι στρυφνὸν, ἔκδηλον δ’ ἑτέραν ποιότητα μηδεμίαν ἔχον, ἐξετάζειν ἐπὶ τῶν τοιούτων ἤδη καὶ βασανίζειν ἀκριβῶς ἐνέργειαν στύψεως. ἡ δὲ ἐξέτασις, ὡς εἴρηται πολλάκις, ἐπί τε τῶν ἐν ἀρίστῃ κατασκευῇ σωμάτων ἀκριβῶς ὑγιαινόντων, ἐπί τε τῶν ἁπλῶν γινέσθω σοι παθῶν, ἤτοιν θερμῶν, ἢ ψυχρῶν, ἢ ξηρῶν, ἢ ὑγρῶν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἤτοιν
Chalkitis, then, and misy 641 and chalkanthos and scale of copper and sory, being at once astringent and biting, act according to both qualities upon each of the bodies with which they come into contact, yet they cannot teach us clearly whether they have their burning on account of the astringency or on account of the pungency. It is better, therefore, to leave these aside and to taste many others in succession, looking for a pure astringency, separately and by itself as far as is at all possible, and when you have found such a one, then at that point to judge the drug by the methods you have often heard before: for instance, if on tasting balaustion, or oak-gall, or kytinos, or hypokistis, or strychnos, or glaukion, or acacia, or juice of sumach, or some other such thing, it should appear to you to be precisely acerbic, while having no other manifest quality, then on such things to examine and test precisely the activity of astringency. And let the examination, as has often been said, be made by you both on bodies in the best constitution that are precisely healthy, and on the simple affections, whether hot, or cold, or dry, or moist.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἤτοιν, ῥοῦ χυλὸς, ἐνέργειαν
Chalcitis, then, and misy 641 and chalcanthus and copper scale and sory, being at once astringent and biting, act according to both qualities upon each of the bodies they come into contact with, but they cannot teach us clearly whether they have their burning through the astringency or through the pungency. It is better, then, to leave these aside and to taste many others in succession, looking for an astringency that is pure, by itself and on its own as far as possible; and when you find such, then at last judge the drug by the methods you have often heard of before. For instance, if on tasting it balaustion, or oak-gall, or cytinus, or hypocistis, or strychnos, or glaucium, or acacia, or the juice of sumach, or some other such thing appears to you to be precisely acerbic and to have no other evident quality, then examine on such things, and test precisely, the action of astringency. And let the examination, as has been said many times, be made by you both on bodies in the best constitution that are perfectly healthy and on the simple affections, whether hot, or cold, or dry, or moist.

K11.641-642K. XI 641–642, ch. 4.7 difficulty high (5.26/100; thin 4, absent 4) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
εἰ μὲν γὰρ αὐτό τε τὸ ὑγιαῖνον 642 σῶμα φαίνοιτο σαφῶς ἐμψῦχον, ἐπί τε τῶν θερμῶν ἁρμόττει παθῶν, ἐναργῆ δὲ καὶ αὐτῷ τῷ κάμνοντι τὴν ψυχρὰν προσβάλλει ποιότητα, τολμᾷν ἤδη τι περὶ τῆς στύψεως ὡς ψυχούσης ἀποφαίνεσθαι. εἰ δ’ ἔμπαλιν ἔχον εὑρίσκοιτο καὶ τῶν ὑγιαινόντων σωμάτων ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ θερμαινομένων καὶ τῶν θερμῶν νοσημάτων παροξυνομένων καὶ τῶν ψυχρῶν ὠφελουμένων οὐ ψύχειν, ἀλλὰ θερμαίνειν ἡγεῖσθαι χρὴ τὸ τοιοῦτον φάρμακον. οὕτω δὲ καὶ περὶ τῶν δριμέων αὐτῶν καθ’ ἑαυτὰ ποιεῖσθαι τὴν βάσανον, ἐπί τε κρόμμυον ἰόντα καὶ σκόροδον καὶ πέπερι καὶ πύρεθρον καὶ ζιγγίβερι καὶ στρούθιον, ἑλένιόν τε καὶ τίτανον. ὥσπερ γὰρ ὁ μίξας στρύχνου χυλῷ πέπερι δοκιμάζειν οὐδέτερον αὐτῶν ἱκανός ἐστιν, οὕτως οὐδ’ ὁ τὰ πρὸς τῆς φύσεως μεμιγμένα. κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον ἀξιῶ σε καὶ τὴν ὀξεῖαν ποιότητα καὶ τὴν πικρὰν καὶ τὴν γλυκεῖαν ἐπισκέπτεσθαι σφοδρὰς καὶ μόνας ὡς οἷόν τ’ ἐστὶν εἰς τὴν δοκιμασίαν ἄγοντα καὶ τῇ πείρᾳ τὰς δυνάμεις αὐτῶν ἐξευρίσκοντα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For if the healthy 642 body itself should appear to be clearly cooled, and it is suited to hot affections, and it imparts a manifest cold quality to the patient himself, then one may at that point venture to declare something about astringency as cooling. But if it should be found to be the opposite, with healthy bodies being heated by it, hot diseases being exacerbated and cold ones benefited, one must consider such a drug not to cool but to heat. In the same way one should also make the test of pungent things themselves by themselves, turning to onion and garlic and pepper and pellitory and ginger and soapwort, elecampane and lime. For just as one who has mixed pepper with juice of strychnos is not competent to test either of them, so neither is one who tests things that have been mixed by nature. And in the same manner I require you to investigate also the sour quality and the bitter and the sweet, bringing them to the test as intense and as isolated as is possible, and discovering their powers by experience.

But if it should be found to behave in the opposite way, healthy bodies being heated by it, hot diseases exacerbated and cold ones benefited, one must consider such a drug not to cool but to heat. Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But to bring to trial everything whatsoever that is acerbic, or harsh, or bitter, as though one were going to obtain in it a test of acerbity, or harshness, 643 or bitterness, is dreadfully absurd. For if it partakes of one quality only, you will have the same trial of the drug and of the quality; but if of several, the drug will then clearly perform an activity mixed from all of them, so that the activity of the single quality under judgment still remains unclear. If, then, one must judge as has been stated before, both those who supposed that it is not possible at all to declare anything about the power of the flavours, and those who made the judgment in another way, supposed incorrectly. For it is both possible to declare something about them, and it is fitting to make the judgment in no other way than as has now been stated. Now if they had said that there is no need at all of the examination concerning the flavours when we investigate the powers of drugs—for it is possible even without these to discover their power by experience—I would have praised rather than blamed them. But since they treat of it as something necessary, yet unknowable, it is just to blame these men on two counts: both because they took what is not necessary to be necessary, and because they despaired in advance of its discovery.

But for me it is perhaps necessary 645 to make some declaration about my own experience, in general terms in the present discourse, and in particular through the treatises that follow. The general account concerning all of them would be as follows. Every astringent body, at least one that is pure of the other qualities, so far as regards perception, I have always found, upon testing, to be cold. Indeed, even some of those that are mixed in their qualities, namely those that are moderately sweet and bitter and pungent, were clearly found to be cooling, since evidently the power of being astringent and of cooling prevails in them, so that the other powers are obscured. By the same reasoning, all sour things too, both those that are simply sour, such as oxalis and oxylapathon, and some of those mixed with other qualities, whether sweet or pungent or bitter, are found no less to be cooling: pomegranates and apples and plums and grapes and figs and mulberries and cherries and vinegar itself. But with this last, as was said before, a certain pungency is mixed in, while with the fruits sweetnesses and acerbities, and in some of them also faint bitternesses.

647 Ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τῶν πικρῶν ἐπὶ πλέον ἐφεξῆς εἰρήσεται. τὸν δὲ περὶ τῶν στυφόντων καὶ ὀξέων αὖθις ἀναλαβόντες λόγον ἐπιθῶμεν ἤδη τὴν προσήκουσαν αὐτῷ τελευτήν. τὸ τοίνυν αὐστηρὸν, ὅτι μὲν ἐκλελυμένον ἐστὶ στρυφνὸν ἀναμνησθῆναι χρὴ τῆς αἰσθήσεως. ἥτις δὲ ἡ γένεσις αὐτοῦ, νῦν εἰρήσεται. ἐπειδὴ τὸ στρυφνὸν σῶμα γεῶδές τε καὶ ψυχρὸν ἦν, ἐκλυθήσεται κατ’ ἀνάγκην τριχῶς, ἢ θερμαινόμενον, ἢ ὑγραινόμενον, ἢ συναμφότερον ἅμα πάσχον ἂν μὲν οὖν θερμαίνηται μόνον, οὐκ ἔστι μὲν οὔθ’ ὑγρότερον οὔτε μαλακώτερον, ἀλλὰ σκληρὸν μένον ἐπικτήσεται γλυκύτητα, καθάπερ αἱ τῶν δρυῶν βάλανοι καὶ μάλιστα αἱ ὀνομαζόμεναι καστανεῖαι. ἐὰν δ’ ὑγραίνηται μόνον, ἐὰν μὲν παχυμερὴς καὶ ὑδατώδης ἡ ὑγρότης, αὐστηρὸν ἀποτελεσθήσεται. παντὸς γὰρ χυμοῦ δύναμιν ἡ ὑδατώδης ὑγρότης ἀμβλύνει. ἐὰν δὲ λεπτομερής τε καὶ ἀερώδης, ὀξύνει. δέδεικται γὰρ ὡς τὸ λεπτομερὲς ψυχρὸν ὀξεῖαν ἀπεργάζεται ποιότητα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐκλελυμένον, χυμοῦ
647 But concerning bitter things more will be said in what follows. Taking up once more the account of astringent and sour things, let us now set upon it the conclusion that befits it. As for the harsh, then, that it is a slackened acerbic one must recall from sensation; but what its genesis is will now be stated. Since the acerbic body was earthy and cold, it will necessarily be slackened in three ways: either by being heated, or by being moistened, or by undergoing both together at the same time. If, then, it is heated only, it is neither moister nor softer, but, remaining hard, it will acquire sweetness in addition, as do the acorns of oaks and especially those called chestnuts. But if it is moistened only, then if the moisture is coarse-particled and watery, it will be rendered harsh; for watery moisture blunts the power of every flavour. But if the moisture is fine-particled and airy, it makes it sour; for it has been shown that what is fine-particled and cold produces a sour quality.

647 But concerning bitter things more will be said in what follows. Let us now take up again the account of astringent and sour things and give it its fitting conclusion. That the harsh is a slackened acerbic, one need only be reminded of what sensation tells us. How it comes into being will now be stated. Since the acerbic body was earthy and cold, it will of necessity be slackened in three ways: either by being heated, or by being moistened, or by undergoing both together at the same time. If, then, it is only heated, it does not become either moister or softer, but, remaining hard, it will acquire sweetness, as do the acorns of oaks, and especially those named chestnuts. But if it is only moistened, then if the moisture is coarse-particled and watery, it will be rendered harsh, for watery moisture blunts the power of every flavour; but if the moisture is fine-particled and airy, it makes it sour. For it has been shown that the fine-particled cold produces a sour quality.

But if it is moistened and heated at the same time, with a watery moisture it will have its transition into sweetness, but with an airy one 648 into oiliness. Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Sweet alone is the Theraean and the Scybellite 649 and the boiled-down must, and all the others that resemble these. Oily, both as fruit and as juice at once, is in the strict sense that of the olive, and indeed also all the others from which oil is prepared, such as that of the castor-plant (kiki) and that of the radish, and the sesame seeds themselves and the nuts and the almonds. For all such things, as they age, become completely oily, since the watery and superfluous moisture in them evaporates away, while the proper moisture is worked up by the innate heat and thoroughly concocted. Those fruits, then, which become oil-like only are both edible and sweet; but those to which some bitterness accrues in the course of the change become inedible. For everything bitter is inedible, and it comes about through an excess of warmth, as we shall demonstrate next.

Sweet only are the Theraean [wine] 649 and the Scybellites and boiled-down must, and whatever else resembles these. Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But everything sweet is hot, and yet does not immoderately exceed the heat that is in us; rather, just as hot water, which we take pleasure in touching, and especially when we have been chilled, is most pleasant and most beneficial so long as it warms us 650 to the point of diffusing the parts of the body that have been congealed by the cold, yet without dissolving or cutting and separating their continuity, so too every sweet food is in every case hot, yet does not reach so great a degree of heat as to cause distress, but remains within the limits of what diffuses, smooths and softens. Recall here again what has often been said by us before, that some things act upon and are acted upon by one another in respect of one or two qualities, others in respect of their whole substances; and among these latter we said there are not a few drugs and all nutriments. The genus of these seems to be twofold, as Theophrastus too indicated. For some are oily, like soft fat, others sweet to a greater or lesser degree, like all the fruits that we eat and the flesh of animals, and indeed also many herbs and roots and flowers and shoots. But everything sweet is hot, and does not immoderately exceed the heat in us.
