ἐγγὺς δὲ τούτων εἰσὶ κατὰ τὴν δύναμιν οἱ Σοδομηνοὶ 695 χόνδροι μὲν ὄντες ὁμοίως τοῖς θαλαττίοις, ἀλλὰ διὰ τὴν ἀναμεμιγμένην αὐτοῖς πικρότητα ῥυπτικώτεροί τε καὶ διαφορητικώτεροι τῶν ἐκ τῆς θαλάττης ὑπάρχοντες. οὕτω δὲ δὴ καὶ τὸ νίτρον αὐτὸ καὶ ἀφρὸς αὐτοῦ καὶ τὸ συνθέτῳ προσηγορίᾳ καλούμενον ἀφρόνιτρον ἔτι μᾶλλον τῶν ἄλλων πέφυκε ῥύπτειν, ὡς ἂν ἥκιστα μὲν στύφοντα, πικρὰ δ’ ἀκριβῶς ὑπάρχοντα. Πλησίον μὲν γάρ ἐστιν ὁ ἁλυκὸς χυμὸς τοῦ πικροῦ, καὶ γὰρ γεώδεις ἀμφότεροι καὶ θερμοί. διαφέρουσι δ’ ὅμως οὐκ ἀσαφεῖ διαφορᾷ ἐν τῷ λεπτύνεσθαί τε καὶ κατειργάσθαι μᾶλλον ὑπὸ θερμότητος ξηρᾶς τὸν πικρόν. οὕτω δὲ δὴ καὶ αὐτῶν τῶν ἁλῶν ὅσοι σκληρότεροι καὶ πυκνότεροι καὶ γεωδέστεροι, τοιοῦτοι δ’ ὑπάρχουσιν ὀλίγου δεῖν οἱ ὀρυκτοὶ πάντες, ἧττον θερμοί τ’ εἰσὶ καὶ λεπτομερεῖς, ὅσοι δ’ εὔθρυπτοι καὶ χαῦνοι, λεπτομερέστεροί τε ἅμα καὶ θερμότεροι τὴν δύναμίν εἰσι, καί τινες ἐξ αὐτῶν ὑπόπικροι, μεταξύ πως ὑπάρχοντες ἁλῶν τε τῶν σκληρῶν καὶ ἀφρονίτρου. ἔστι μὲν οὖν δὴ καὶ αὐτῶν τῶν ἀφρονίτρων ἔνια σκληρὰ καὶ παχυμερῆ, καὶ οὐδὲ τήκεται ῥᾳδίως ἐφ’ ὕδατος.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χόνδροι
Close to these in power are the Sodomene salts, 695 being in lumps like the sea salts, but on account of the bitterness mixed in with them being more cleansing and more dispersing than those from the sea. So too nitre itself and its foam, and what is called by the composite name aphronitron, are by nature even more cleansing than the others, inasmuch as they are least astringent while being precisely bitter. For the salty flavour is near to the bitter, since both are earthy and hot; yet they differ by no obscure difference, in that the bitter has been thinned and worked up more by dry heat. So too, of the salts themselves, those that are harder and denser and more earthy—and such are almost all the mined salts—are less hot and fine-particled, whereas those that are friable and loose-textured are at once both more fine-particled and hotter in power, and some of them are somewhat bitter, being in a way intermediate between the hard salts and aphronitron. There are indeed also some of the aphronitra themselves that are hard and coarse-particled, and do not even dissolve easily in water.

Close to these in power are the Sodomene salts, 695 which are coarse-grained like the sea salts, but because of the bitterness mixed into them are more cleansing and more dispersing than those from the sea. In the same way nitre itself, its foam, and what is called by a compound name aphronitre are by nature still more cleansing than the others, inasmuch as they are least astringent and thoroughly bitter. For the salty flavour is close to the bitter, since both are earthy and hot. Nevertheless they differ by no obscure difference, in that the bitter has been thinned and worked up more by dry heat. In the same way, among the salts themselves, those that are harder, denser and more earthy (and almost all the mined salts are of this kind) are less hot and less fine-particled, whereas those that are easily crumbled and porous are at once more fine-particled and hotter in power, and some of them are slightly bitter, standing in a way between the hard salts and aphronitre. Now even among the aphronitres themselves there are some that are hard and coarse-particled, and they do not even dissolve readily in water.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But 696 my account now is not about the defective ones, but about those which, in accordance with their name, are naturally foam-like in their substance, such as is also the foam of nitre itself. For this is the most fine-particled of all those mentioned, just as is also the flower of the Assian stone. For this too is sufficiently fine-particled, but less hot than aphronitron. Wherefore it also melts away overgrowing flesh with less pain, whereas the flower of nitre already bites it, and aphronitron bites too, and no less nitre itself, even though it is less hot than those. But it has been said before as well that the coarse-particled substance erodes more, when, that is to say, it partakes of some heat. For no part of it passes through painlessly and quickly, as that of the fine-particled does, but being held back and always remaining and lingering, and sitting for a longer time upon one place of our body, it naturally becomes painful, like a thorn fixed in the flesh. Since what is fitting has been said about all the flavours, it would remain still to speak also about the vapours.

But 696 my discussion now is not about the poor-quality ones, but about those which, in accordance with the name, are foamy in their substance by nature, such as is the foam of nitre itself. For this is the most fine-particled of all those mentioned, just as is also the flower of the Asian stone. For this too is sufficiently fine-particled, but less hot than aphronitre. Hence it also melts away overgrown flesh more painlessly, whereas the flower of nitre already bites it, and aphronitre bites it too, and no less does nitre itself, even though it is less hot than those. But it has also been said earlier that coarse-particled substance corrodes more, provided, that is, that it partakes of some heat. For no part of it passes through painlessly and quickly, as happens with fine-particled substance; instead, being held back, always remaining and lingering, and staying for a longer time at one place in our body, it reasonably becomes distressing, like a stake fixed in the flesh. Since what is fitting has been said about all the flavours, it would remain still to speak also about vapours.

Greek (Kühn)
ἐπὶ 697 τούτῳ γὰρ τῷ γένει τῶν αἰσθητικῶν τὴν τῆς ὀσμῆς αἴσθησιν ἡ φύσις ἐδημιούργησεν. οἱ μὲν δὴ πλεῖστοι τοῖς χυμοῖς αὐτοῖς ὁμοίως ἡμᾶς διατιθέασιν. τά τε γὰρ ὀξέα πάντα καὶ πρὸ τούτων ὄξος αὐτὸ τὴν ὄσφρησίν τε καὶ τὴν γεῦσιν ὡσαύτως κινεῖ, τά τε δριμέα, καθάπερ σκόροδα καὶ κρόμμυα, καὶ ταῦτ’ οὐδὲν ἧττον τῆς γεύσεως ἀνιᾷ τε καὶ δάκνει τὴν ὄσφρησιν. οὕτω δὲ καὶ καθ’ ἕκαστον τῶν ἄλλων ὁμοία σχεδὸν ἡ τῆς ὀσμῆς αἴσθησίς ἐστι τῇ τῆς γεύσεως, ὥστε ἐνίων σωμάτων οὐδὲ γευσάμενοί ποτε, καθάπερ τῆς κόπρου, γιγνώσκομεν τὴν ποιότητα, καὶ κατὰ τοῦτό γε τὴν ἀρχὴν οὐδ’ ἐπιχειροῦμεν γεύεσθαι, τῷ πάνυ σφόδρα πιστεύειν τῇ ὀσφρήσει. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τῶν εὐωδῶν ἐδεσμάτων ὅσα διασαπέντα κατὰ τὴν ὀσμὴν ἡμᾶς ἀνιᾷ, καὶ ταῦτ’ εὐθέως ἀποῤῥιπτοῦμεν, οὐδ’ ἐπιχειροῦντες γεύεσθαι, καὶ σχεδὸν ἐπὶ πάντων ὁμολογοῦσιν ἀλλήλαις ὄσφρησίς τε καὶ γεῦσις. ἐπὶ μέντοι τῶν ἡδίστων, οἷά περ καὶ τὰ ῥόδα, διαφέρουσί τε καὶ πάμπολυ διεστήκασιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: αἰσθητικῶν
For it is for this class 697 of sensible things (αἰσθητικῶν) that nature fashioned the sense of smell. Now most of them affect us in the same way as the flavours themselves. For all sour things, and before these vinegar itself, stir the sense of smell and the sense of taste alike; and the pungent ones, such as garlic and onions, these too distress and bite the sense of smell no less than the sense of taste. And so too in each of the other cases the sensation of smell is almost the same as that of taste, so that in the case of some bodies we recognise the quality without ever having tasted them, as with dung, and for this very reason we do not even attempt to taste them in the first place, because we trust the sense of smell so very strongly. And indeed, those fragrant foods which, having putrefied, distress us by their smell, these too we at once throw away without even attempting to taste them; and in almost all cases smell and taste agree with one another. In the case of the most pleasant things, however, such as roses, they differ and stand very far apart.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: αἰσθητικῶν (one would expect αἰσθητῶν, 'sensible objects'; rendered so), χυμοῖς (rendered 'flavours'; could be 'juices')
For 697 it is for this genus of sensible objects that nature fashioned the sensation of odour. Most of them affect us in the same way as the flavours themselves. For all sour things, and before these vinegar itself, move the sense of smell and the taste alike; and pungent things, such as garlic and onions, these too, no less than they do the taste, distress and bite the sense of smell. So too in each of the others the sensation of odour is nearly similar to that of taste, so that of some bodies, such as dung, we know the quality without ever having tasted them, and for this reason we do not even attempt to taste them in the first place, because we trust the sense of smell very strongly indeed. Moreover, of fragrant foods, those that have putrefied and distress us by their odour we likewise throw away at once, not even attempting to taste them; and in nearly all cases smell and taste agree with one another. In the case of the most pleasant things, however, such as roses, they differ and stand very far apart.

οὐ μόνον γὰρ οὐχ ἡδέα γευομένοις ἐστὶν, ἀλλὰ καὶ πικρότητος οὐκ ὀλίγον 698 ἐμφαίνει. καίτοι τόγε πικρὸν οὐδέποτ’ ἦν εὐῶδες οὐδ’ ὡς φάρμακον, οὐδ’ ὡς τροφὴ ἡμῖν ἡδύ. τίς οὖν ἡ τῆς διαφωνίας ταύτης αἰτία, διὸ καὶ Θεόφραστος μὲν ἐζήτησεν, ἄμεινον δ’ ἴσως καὶ ἡμᾶς ὅσα γιγνώσκομεν ὑπὲρ αὐτῆς εἰπεῖν. ἡ τοῦ πικροῦ γένεσις χυμοῦ ἐκ τῶν γλυκέων ὑπὸ θερμότητος λεπτυνομένων ἐφαίνετο συνίστασθαι καὶ εἶναι δηλονότι θερμότερά τε καὶ λεπτομερέστερα τῇ κράσει τὰ πικρὰ τῶν γλυκέων, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἡ τῆς ὀσμῆς αἴσθησις ἔν τε ταῖς κοιλίαις αὐταῖς γίγνεται τοῦ ἐγκεφάλου, καθότι καὶ δέδεικταί μοι περὶ τούτων ἑτέρωθι, καὶ οὐσία τῶν ὀσφρητῶν ἀτμώδης ἐστί. τὰ γὰρ ἀποῤῥέοντα τῶν σωμάτων ἀναμιγνύμενα τῷ περιέχοντι, κᾄπειτα διὰ τῆς κατὰ τὰς ῥῖνας εἰσπνοῆς εἰς τὸν ἐγκέφαλον ἐνεχθέντα, κινεῖ τὴν αἴσθησιν. εὐλόγως οὖν ὅσα μὲν ὀσμώδη πάντα καὶ θερμά· τὸ γὰρ τῶν ἀτμῶν πλῆθος ἐπὶ θερμότητι· οὐ μὴν εὐθὺς καὶ πᾶν ὀσμῶδες ἡδὺ, διότι μηδ’ οἰκεῖόν ἐστιν ἅπαν τοῦ κατὰ τὰς κοιλίας τοῦ ἐγκεφάλου πνεύματος.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For not only are they not pleasant to those who taste them, but they even display no small degree of bitterness. 698 And yet the bitter was never fragrant, nor pleasant to us either as a drug or as food. What, then, is the cause of this disagreement — a question which Theophrastus too investigated; but perhaps it is better that we also say what we know about it. The generation of the bitter flavour appeared to arise from sweet things being thinned by heat, and bitter things are evidently hotter and more fine-particled in mixture than sweet things; but, further, the sensation of smell occurs in the very ventricles of the brain, as has also been demonstrated by me elsewhere concerning these matters, and the substance of odorous objects is vaporous. For the effluences from bodies, mingling with the surrounding air and then carried into the brain through the inhalation that takes place by way of the nostrils, stir the sensation. Reasonably, then, all things that are odorous are also hot — for the abundance of vapours depends on heat; yet not everything odorous is thereby also pleasant, because not everything is akin to the pneuma in the ventricles of the brain.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διὸ (in 'διὸ καὶ Θεόφραστος μὲν ἐζήτησεν'; possibly corrupt for ὃ or ἣν, 'which'; the syntax of the sentence is loose), τῷ περιέχοντι (rendered 'the surrounding air'), ἐπὶ θερμότητι (rendered 'depends on heat')
For not only are they not pleasant to those who taste them, but they also display no small amount 698 of bitterness. And yet the bitter was never fragrant, nor pleasant to us either as a drug or as a food. What, then, is the cause of this disagreement? Theophrastus too inquired into it, but it is perhaps better that we also say what we know about it. The generation of the bitter flavour was shown to come about from sweet things thinned by heat, and bitter things are evidently hotter and more fine-particled in their mixture than sweet things. Moreover, the sensation of smell takes place in the very ventricles of the brain, as I have demonstrated about these matters elsewhere, and the substance of the objects of smell is vaporous. For the things that flow off from bodies, mixing with the surrounding air and then being carried into the brain by inhalation through the nostrils, move the sense. Reasonably, then, all things that are odorous are also hot, for an abundance of vapours depends on heat. Yet it does not follow at once that everything odorous is also pleasant, because not everything is akin to the pneuma in the ventricles of the brain.

Greek (Kühn)
ὥσπερ γὰρ τῶν προσπιπτόντων τῇ ﻿ 699 γλώττῃ χυμῶν οἱ μὲν οἰκειότατοι γλυκεῖς ἦσαν, οἱ δ’ οὐκ οἰκεῖοι πολλὰς ἐκέκτηντο διαφορὰς, οὕτω καὶ τῶν ἀτμῶν οἱ μὲν οἰκεῖοι τῷ κατὰ τὸν ἐγκέφαλον πνεύματι φίλιοί τ’ εἰσὶ καὶ ἡδεῖς, οἱ δ’ οὐκ οἰκεῖοι διαφέρουσι μὲν ἀλλήλων οὐκ ὀλίγαις διαφοραῖς, ὀνόματα δ’ αὐταῖς ἁπάσαις οὐ κεῖται καθάπερ ἐπὶ τῶν χυμῶν. ὀξεῖαν μὲν γάρ τινα καὶ δριμεῖαν ὀσμὴν ἔχειν τόδε τί φαμεν, αὐστηρὰν δὲ ἢ στρυφνὴν ἢ ἁλυκὴν, ἢ πικρὰν οὐκέτι λέγομεν, ἀλλ’ εἰς δύο ταύτας ἀνάγομεν προσηγορίας τὰ πλεῖστα τῶν ὀσφρητῶν, εὐώδη τε καὶ δυσώδη προσαγορεύοντες· εὐώδη μὲν ἀνάλογον τοῖς πρὸς τὴν γλῶτταν γλυκέσι, δυσώδη δὲ τοῖς μὴ γλυκέσιν. ἀνώνυμον γὰρ, ὅσον ἐπί γε τῶν χυμῶν ἐστιν, ἑνὶ προσρήματι τὸ τούτων γένος. ἔοικε δὲ τῶν οὐκ ὀσμωδῶν σωμάτων ἤτοι παντελῶς ὀλίγον ἀποῤῥεῖν, ἢ τοῖς ὄγκοις ἀσύμμετρον, ὡς ἐπὶ τῶν ἀκριβῶς ἁλυκῶν καὶ στρυφνῶν. ἀκριβῶς δ’ ὅταν εἴπω τι τοῖον ἢ τοῖον ὑπάρχειν, εἰλικρινὲς καὶ ἄμικτον αὐτὸ, καθόσον οἷόν τε ποιότητος ἑτέρας εἶναι φημί.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τοῖς ὄγκοις ἀσύμμετρον, ὅσον ἐπί γε τῶν χυμῶν ἐστιν
For just as, among the flavours that fall upon the 699 tongue, the most proper were the sweet ones, while those that are not proper possessed many differences, so too among the vapours those proper to the pneuma in the brain are friendly and pleasant, whereas those not proper differ from one another by not a few differences, but names are not assigned to all of these, as they are in the case of the flavours. For we say that this or that thing has a certain sour and pungent odour, but we no longer say a harsh or acerbic or salty or bitter one; rather we refer most of the objects of smell to these two appellations, calling them fragrant and foul-smelling: fragrant by analogy with the things that are sweet to the tongue, foul-smelling by analogy with those that are not sweet. For the genus of these latter, so far at least as the flavours are concerned, has no name under a single designation. And it seems that from bodies which are not odorous either altogether little flows off, or what flows off is incommensurate in its particles, as in the case of the precisely salty and the acerbic. And whenever I say that something is precisely such or such, I mean that it is pure and unmixed, so far as is possible, with any other quality.

For just as, of the flavours that fall upon the 699 tongue, the most proper were sweet, while those that were not proper possessed many differences, so too of the vapours, those proper to the pneuma in the brain are friendly and pleasant, while those that are not proper differ from one another by not a few differences, yet names have not been laid down for all of these, as they have in the case of the flavours. For we do say that this or that thing has a sour and pungent smell, but we no longer speak of a harsh or acerbic or salty or bitter one; rather, we reduce most objects of smell to these two designations, calling them fragrant and malodorous: fragrant by analogy with the things that are sweet to the tongue, malodorous by analogy with those that are not sweet. For, so far as the flavours are concerned, the class of these latter has no name expressed by a single appellation. It seems that from bodies that are not odorous either altogether little flows off, or what flows off is incommensurate in its particles, as in the case of things that are strictly salty and acerbic. And whenever I say that something is strictly of this or that kind, I mean that it is pure and unmixed with another quality, so far as that is possible.

Greek (Kühn)
παχυμερής τε γὰρ ἀμφοῖν ἡ οὐσία καὶ πρὸς τούτῳ ψυχρὰ τῶν στρυφνῶν, 700 ὥστ’ εἰκὸς καὶ τὸ ἀποῤῥέον αὐτῶν ὀλίγον τε εἶναι καὶ παχὺ καὶ οἷον γεῶδες τοῖς ὄγκοις, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μηδ’ ἐμπίπτειν ἐν ταῖς ἀναπνοαῖς εἰς τὸν ἐγκέφαλον. Ὅθεν οὐδ’ ἀσφαλὲς ἐξ ὀσμῆς τεκμαίρεσθαι περὶ τῆς κράσεως τῶν αἰσθητῶν, ὥσπερ ἐκ τῆς γεύσεως. τὰ μὲν γὰρ ἄοσμα παχυμερῆ ταῖς οὐσίαις, ἀλλ’ οὔπω δῆλον ὅπως ἔχῃ θερμότητος καὶ ψύξεως, τὰ δ’ ὀσμώδη λεπτομερῆ τέ πώς ἐστι καὶ θερμά. τὸ μέντοι ποσὸν τῆς λεπτομερείας ἢ τῆς θερμότητος οὐκέτ’ ἐνδείκνυται. τὸ δὲ δὴ μέγιστον ἅπασι τοῖς ὀσμώδεσιν εἰς τὸ μηδὲν ἐνδείκνυσθαι σαφὲς ὑπὲρ τῆς κράσεως ἡ ἀνωμαλία τῆς οὐσίας ἐστὶν, ὑπὲρ ἧς εἴρηταί μοι πολλάκις ἤδη, δεικνύοντι τὰ πλεῖστα τῶν σωμάτων ἀνομοιομερῆ ταῖς ὑποστάσεσιν ὑπάρχοντα. λεχθήσεται δὲ καὶ νῦν ἕνεκα σαφηνείας ἕν τι παράδειγμα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὄγκοις, ὑποστάσεσιν
For the substance of both is coarse-particled, and in addition that of the acerbic ones is cold, 700 so that it is likely that what flows off from them is both scanty and thick and, as it were, earthy in its masses, and for this reason does not even enter the brain in the acts of breathing. Hence it is not safe to draw inferences from smell about the mixture of perceptible things, as it is from taste. For odourless things are coarse-particled in their substances, but it is not yet clear how they stand in respect of heat and cold; whereas odorous things are in some way fine-particled and hot. Yet the amount of the fineness of particles or of the heat is no longer indicated. But indeed the greatest factor, for all odorous things, in their indicating nothing clear about the mixture is the unevenness of their substance, about which I have already spoken many times, showing that most bodies are non-homoeomerous in their constitution. And now too, for the sake of clarity, one example will be stated.

For the substance of both is coarse-particled, and in addition that of the acerbic ones is cold, 700 so that it is likely that what flows off from them is small in quantity, thick, and as it were earthy in its corpuscles, and for this reason does not even enter the brain in respiration. Greek (Kühn)
τὸ ῥόδον τοίνυν οὐ ταύτῃ μόνον ἀνομοιομερές ἐστιν, ᾗ τὸ μέν τι περιέχον ἐν αὐτῷ, τὸ δέ τι περιεχόμενόν ἐστι, περιέχον μὲν τὸ σκληρὸν καὶ γεῶδες, ἐν τούτῳ δὲ περιεχόμενος 701 ὑγρός τις χυλός. ἀλλ’ ὅτι καὶ αὐτοῦ τοῦ χυλοῦ τρία περιττώματα, καθότι καὶ πρόσθεν ἐδείκνυμεν, ἀναγκαῖον ὑπάρχειν, ἓν μὲν γεῶδες, οἷόν περ ἐν τοῖς οἴνοις ἡ τρὺξ, ἕτερον δὲ ἀερῶδες, ἀνάλογον δὲ καὶ τοῦτο τῷ κατὰ τοὺς οἴνους ἄνθει, τὸ τρίτον ὑδατῶδες, ὅπερ ἐν ἅπασι τὴν ζέσιν αὐτῶν ἔφαμεν ἐργάζεσθαι· καὶ τὴν φθορὰν, ἐπειδὰν μὲν μὴ κρατηθῇ μηδ’ ἀλλοιωθῇ τελέως ἐν τῷ χρόνῳ τῆς ζέσεως. ἕνεκά γε τοιούτου τοῦ περιττώματος ἔνιοι μὲν ἑψήσαντες ἀποτίθενται τοὺς χυλοὺς, ἔνιοι δ’ ἐν ἡλίῳ σφοδρῷ προξηράναντες. ἕνεκα μὲν γὰρ τοῦ γεώδους τε καὶ ἀερώδους οὐ πάνυ τι δεδίασι φθαρῆναι τοὺς χυλοὺς, ὁρῶντες αὐτὰ καὶ διακρινόμενα καὶ δύσσηπτα ταῖς οὐσίαις ὑπάρχοντα, διότι καὶ ξηρὰ ταῖς κράσεσιν. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τούτων ἱκανῶς εἴρηται πρόσθεν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἄνθει
The rose, then, is non-homoeomerous not only in this respect, that one thing in it is what contains and another what is contained — what contains being the hard and earthy part, and what is contained within this 701 being a certain moist juice — but also in that there must necessarily be, as we showed before, three residues of the juice itself: one earthy, just like the lees in wines; another airy, this one being analogous to the 'flower' in wines; and the third watery, which we said in all of them produces their boiling — and also their corruption, whenever it is not mastered and not completely altered during the time of the boiling. It is on account of this kind of residue that some people store away the juices after boiling them, others after first drying them in a strong sun. For on account of the earthy and the airy residue they are not at all afraid that the juices will be corrupted, seeing that these both separate out and are resistant to putrefaction in their substances, because they are also dry in their mixtures. But about these matters enough has been said before.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἄνθει, καὶ τὴν φθορὰν, ἐπειδὰν μὲν μὴ κρατηθῇ, διακρινόμενα
The rose, then, is anhomoeomerous not only in this respect, that one part in it is containing and another contained, the containing part being the hard and earthy, and contained within this 701 a certain moist juice. It is also anhomoeomerous because the juice itself must necessarily have three residues, as we also showed earlier. One is earthy, like the lees in wines. Another is airy, and this too is analogous to the flower in wines. The third is watery, which we said produces the boiling in all of them, and also their corruption, whenever it is not mastered nor completely altered during the time of the boiling. It is on account of such a residue that some people boil the juices before storing them, and others first dry them in strong sun. For on account of the earthy and the airy residues they are not much afraid that the juices will spoil, since they see that these both separate out and are hard to putrefy in their substances, because they are also dry in their mixtures. But enough has been said about these matters earlier.

Greek (Kühn)
ἐν δὲ τῇ τοῦ ῥόδου φύσει τὸ μέν ἐστι στρυφνὸν γευομένοις, ὅπερ ἀναγκαῖον εἶναι γεῶδές τε καὶ παχυμερὲς καὶ ψυχρὸν, ἕτερον δὲ πικρὸν, ὃ δὴ λεπτομερές τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ θερμὸν, καὶ τρίτον ἐπὶ τούτοις τὸ ὑδατῶδες, ψυχρὸν μὲν ἐξ ἀνάγκης, ἀλλ’ ἐν τῇ κατὰ τὸ λεπτομερές τε καὶ παχυμερὲς 702 ἀντιθέσει μέσον πως ὑπάρχον τῶν ἄκρων, οὗ τῇ μίξει καὶ τὸ στρυφνὸν καὶ τὸ πικρὸν οὐκ ἔστιν ἄκρως τοιοῦτον ἑκάτερον οἷόν περ λέγεται· πάσας γὰρ ἐκλύει τὰς ποιότητας ἡ τοῦ ὑδατώδους ἐπιμιξία. τοῦτ’ οὖν αὐτὸ τὸ ὑδατῶδες ἔοικέ πως ἤδη τε καὶ κατειργάσθαι καὶ λελεπτύνθαι καὶ τεθερμάνθαι, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ῥᾳδίως εἰς ἀτμοὺς ἄγεσθαι καὶ ταύτῃ τὸ ῥόδον ὀσμῶδές τε εἶναι καὶ ξηραίνεσθαι τάχιστα. καὶ ταῦθ’ ὑπὲρ αὐτοῦ πάντα διὰ μὲν τῆς γεύσεως ἔνεστι τεκμήρασθαι, διὰ δὲ τῆς ὀσμῆς οὐχ οἷόν τε τῷ μήθ’ ὁμοίως ἀτμίζειν ἅπαντ’ αὐτοῦ τὰ μόρια μήθ’ ὁμοίως κινεῖν τὴν αἴσθησιν. ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς γεύσεως ἅπανθ’ ὁμοίως τὰ μόρια τῶν γευστῶν σωμάτων προσπίπτει τῇ γλώττῃ καὶ κινεῖ τὴν αἴσθησιν ἕκαστον αὐτῶν κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν, ὥστ’ οὐκ ἀσφαλὲς, ὡς εἴρηται, περὶ τῆς τῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως ἁπάσης ἐξ ὀσμῆς τεκμαίρεσθαι. ταυτὶ γὰρ μόνα διαγνῶναι δυνατὸν, ὅσα περ εἴρηται νῦν. ἔτι δὲ μᾶλλον οὐδ’ ἐκ τῶν χρωμάτων ἔνεστι στοχάσασθαί τι περὶ τῆς τῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But in the nature of the rose there is one part that is acerbic to those who taste it, which must necessarily be earthy, coarse-particled and cold; another that is bitter, which is in fact fine-particled and hot; and a third besides these, the watery, cold of necessity, but in the 702 opposition between fine-particled and coarse-particled standing somehow midway between the extremes; and by the mixture of this neither the acerbic nor the bitter is, either of them, such in the extreme degree as it is said to be, for the admixture of the watery relaxes all the qualities. This watery part itself, then, seems somehow already to have been worked up and made thin and heated, and on this account to be easily brought into vapours, and in this way the rose is both odorous and dries very quickly. And all these things about it can be inferred through taste, but through smell it is not possible, because neither do all its parts give off vapour alike nor move the sense alike. In the case of taste, on the other hand, all the parts of tastable bodies alike fall upon the tongue, and each of them moves the sense according to its own nature; so that it is not safe, as has been said, to make inferences from smell about the whole power of drugs. For only these things can be discerned, as many as have just now been stated. And still more, it is not possible to conjecture anything from colours either about the power of drugs.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κατειργάσθαι, ἐκλύει, ταυτὶ γὰρ μόνα διαγνῶναι δυνατὸν
In the nature of the rose there is, to those who taste it, one component that is acerbic, which must necessarily be earthy, coarse-particled and cold; another that is bitter, which is fine-particled and hot; and a third besides these, the watery, which is of necessity cold, but in the opposition between fine-particled and coarse-particled 702 lies somehow midway between the extremes. Because this is mixed in, neither the acerbic nor the bitter is in the extreme degree what it is called, for the admixture of the watery weakens all the qualities. This watery component itself, then, seems somehow to have already been worked up, thinned and heated, and for this reason to be readily brought into vapours; and it is in this way that the rose is both odorous and dries out very quickly. All these things about it can be inferred through taste, but not through smell, because neither do all its parts give off vapour alike nor do they move the sense alike. In taste, by contrast, all the parts of tastable bodies alike fall upon the tongue, and each of them moves the sense according to its own nature. So it is not safe, as has been said, to infer the whole power of drugs from smell, for only such things as have just now been stated can be discerned by it. Still less is it possible to conjecture anything about the power of drugs from their colours.

Greek (Kühn)
καθ’ ἑκάστην γὰρ χρόαν εὑρίσκεται καὶ θερμὰ καὶ ψυχρὰ καὶ ξηρὰ καὶ ὑγρὰ, καθότι κᾀν τοῖς πρώτοις ὑπομνήμασι 703 τῆσδε τῆς πραγματείας ἀποδέδεικταί μοι. καθ’ ἕκαστον μέντοι γένος ἢ σπέρματος ἢ ῥίζης ἢ χυλοῦ δυνατόν ἐστι κᾀκ τῆς χρόας ἔνδειξίν τινα λαβεῖν τῆς κράσεως, οἷον αὐτίκα καὶ κρόμμυον καὶ σκίλλα καὶ οἶνος, εἰς ὅσον ἂν ᾖ λευκότερα καὶ ἧττόν ἐστι θερμά. τὰ δ’ ὑπόξανθά τε καὶ κιῤῥὰ θερμότερα, καὶ πυροὶ δὲ καὶ κέγχροι καὶ ὦχροι καὶ φάσηλοι καὶ ἐρέβινθοι καὶ ἡ τῆς ἴρεως ῥίζα καὶ ἡ τοῦ ἀσφοδέλου καὶ πολλῶν ἄλλων ὅμοιόν τι πεπόνθασιν. ἐν ἑκάστῳ γὰρ γένει τοὐπίπαν, ὅσα κιῤῥὰ καὶ ξανθὰ καὶ ἐρυθρὰ, θερμότερα τῶν λευκῶν ἐστιν, ὥστ’ εἴ τι κᾀντεῦθεν ἐγχωρεῖ περὶ φαρμάκων δυνάμεως τεκμαίρεσθαι, προσκείσθω τῷ λόγῳ κάλλιστον μὲν, ὡς εἴρηταί τε καὶ λέλεκται πολλάκις, ἐκ τῆς διωρισμένης πείρας ἐξευρίσκειν τὰς δυνάμεις. οὐ γὰρ ἂν σφαλείης οὐδὲ ἐν τῇδε, πρὶν μέντοι τῇ πείρᾳ διαγνῶναι τὴν δύναμιν, ἡ γεῦσις ἐκδείκνυται τὰ πολλὰ, συνεπιμαρτυρούσης, ὡς εἴρηται, βραχέα καὶ τῆς ὀσμῆς.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὦχροι, φάσηλοι
For in each colour there are found both hot and cold and dry and moist things, as has been demonstrated by me also in the first books 703 of this treatise. Within each kind, however, whether of seed or of root or of juice, it is possible to obtain from the colour too some indication of the mixture; for example, straightaway, onion and squill and wine, to the extent that they are whiter, are also less hot, while the yellowish and tawny ones are hotter; and wheat and millet and ochri and phaseli and chickpeas and the root of iris and that of asphodel and of many others are affected in a similar way. For in each kind, for the most part, those that are tawny and yellow and red are hotter than the white ones; so that, if it is permissible to infer something from this source too about the power of drugs, let it be added to the account. But the best course, as has been said and stated many times, is to discover the powers from qualified experience; for you would not go wrong in this either. Before, however, one has discerned the power by experience, taste displays most things, with smell too, as has been said, bearing some small additional witness.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὦχροι, φάσηλοι, οὐ γὰρ ἂν σφαλείης οὐδὲ ἐν τῇδε, εἴρηταί τε καὶ λέλεκται
For in every colour there are found things hot and cold and dry and moist, as has been demonstrated by me in the first books 703 of this treatise. Within each kind, however, whether of seed or of root or of juice, it is possible to obtain from the colour as well some indication of the mixture. For example, onions and squill and wine: the whiter they are, the less hot they are, while the yellowish and tawny ones are hotter. Wheat, millet, ochroi, phaseloi, chickpeas, the root of the iris, that of the asphodel and those of many other plants are affected in a similar way. For in each kind, on the whole, those that are tawny, yellow and red are hotter than the white ones. So if anything can be inferred from this source too about the power of drugs, let it be added to the account. Best of all, however, as has been said and stated many times, is to discover the powers from qualified experience, for you would not go wrong even in this case. Before recognising the power by experience, however, taste reveals most things, with smell, as has been said, also bearing some slight additional witness.

704 GALEN, ON THE MIXTURE AND POWER OF SIMPLE DRUGS, BOOK 5. Having undertaken this fifth discourse on the power of simple drugs, I shall first recall those things demonstrated by me in the preceding books that are useful for the present matters, making my beginning from the elements, water and fire, air and earth, which some people, naming them derivatively from the qualities, say are moist and dry and hot and cold.
